| FR-442 | requirement |  | OpenTelemetry tracing spans the API request, the queue hop, the worker, and `pricing-core` calls, with span attributes carrying artifact references. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-443 | requirement |  | Metrics exposed in Prometheus format: request rate/latency/error by route, scoring latency percentiles by environment and rating version, job queue depth and duration by kind, cache hit rate, and blob store usage. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-444 | requirement |  | Health endpoints: `/healthz` (liveness), `/readyz` (readiness including database, Redis, and blob store reachability), and `/version` (image tag, commit, schema version). The scoring service's readiness additionally requires a compiled bundle in cache. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-445 | requirement |  | Errors are reported with the `trace_id` visible to the user, so a support conversation starts with an identifier rather than a screenshot. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-446 | requirement |  | Settings resolve by precedence: **environment variable → workspace setting → platform default**. The effective value and its source are inspectable by an Admin. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-447 | requirement |  | Settings are typed and validated at startup; an invalid setting prevents startup with a clear message rather than failing at first use. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-448 | requirement |  | Workspace settings include: currency, locale/timezone for display, default validation thresholds, trace sampling rate, approval policy reference, retention windows, model complexity thresholds (`modelling.max_factor_count`, `modelling.min_exposure_per_parameter` — both unset by default, `02` FR-185), and feature flags (e.g. `sql_validation_check_enabled`, `expression_objectives_enabled`). **`rating.batch_abort_failure_rate` joins the list, unset by default** *(added 2026-08-29, WK-671 Slice 3. `03` FR-255's "declared threshold" had no home anywhere in the suite — swept, and FR-255 is its only mention. Unset by default for the reason the two model-complexity thresholds above are: the requirement reads "does not abort … unless the failure rate exceeds a declared threshold", so an undeclared one means no rate-based abort, with the counts-and-samples half unaffected. A batch run may pass a value of its own, but only one that **lowers** the effective threshold; that argument is a Job parameter recorded under FR-410, never a fourth tier in FR-446's precedence. Ruled in `docs/rulings/RL-00889-d3-the-batch-abort-threshold-is-a-workspace-setting-with-a-one-directional-per-run-argument.md` RL-889.)* |  |  |  |  |
| FR-449 | requirement |  | Feature flags gate genuinely optional or risk-bearing capabilities (`01` §4.5, `02` FR-150) and default to the safe value. `expression_objectives_enabled` stays off for the whole of Phase 1, because the capability behind it does not exist until Phase 2 (OQ-573, decided 2026-08-15). |  |  |  |  |
| FR-450 | requirement |  | The API implements `00` §5 exactly: `/api/v1`, cursor pagination, RFC 9457 problem responses with stable `code`s, `If-Match` optimistic concurrency, and `Idempotency-Key` support. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-451 | requirement |  | OpenAPI 3.1 is generated from the Pydantic models (ADR-704) and published at `/openapi.json`; the committed copy in `docs/contracts/openapi/` is regenerated in CI and a drift fails the build. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-452 | requirement |  | Rate limiting is applied per Principal and per environment, with scoring limits configured separately from management-API limits, and `429` responses carrying `Retry-After`. |  |  |  |  |
| FR-453 | requirement |  | Webhooks (alert routing, deployment notifications) are signed with an HMAC over the payload, delivered with retries and exponential backoff, and their delivery status is observable. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-454 | requirement |  | Real-time scoring: p99 < 50 ms server-side for a single quote against a live Rating Version, excluding network, at 200 rps sustained per replica. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-455 | requirement |  | Batch scoring throughput: ≥ 1 M risks/hour per worker for a typical motor rating structure (≈ 200 DAG steps). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-456 | requirement |  | GLM fitting: 5 M rows × 60 factors converges in < 10 min on a 16-core worker. GBM fitting: same data, 500 trees, < 20 min. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-457 | requirement |  | Interactive UI: p95 < 300 ms for metadata reads; any operation exceeding 2 s becomes a Job with progress. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-458 | requirement |  | Audit completeness: 100 % of governed state transitions produce an Audit Event; the audit log is append-only at the database privilege level. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-459 | requirement |  | Retention: artifacts and audit events retained ≥ 7 years (UK regulatory expectation); scoring traces sampled and retained ≥ 13 months. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-460 | requirement |  | Recoverability: RPO ≤ 15 min, RTO ≤ 4 h for the pricing metadata database; the blob store is versioned and replicated. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-461 | requirement |  | Security: all traffic TLS 1.3; secrets from environment/secret manager only; no credentials in artifacts; dependency and container scanning in CI. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-462 | requirement |  | Portability: the full stack runs locally via `docker compose up` with no cloud dependency, using MinIO for object storage. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-463 | requirement |  | Accessibility: the SPA meets WCAG 2.2 AA; all charts have an accessible tabular equivalent. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-464 | requirement |  | Licensing: every runtime dependency must be OSI-approved and compatible with the project licence; copyleft-incompatible or source-available-only dependencies are rejected (see `open-questions.md` OQ-541 for the licence choice). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-465 | requirement |  | Ingest + prepare 10 M rows × 80 columns from parquet in ≤ 15 min on a 16-core worker; from CSV in ≤ 30 min. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-466 | requirement |  | Full validation of a 10 M-row version with ~50 rules completes in ≤ 10 min; structural layer alone in ≤ 2 min so it can fail fast. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-467 | requirement |  | Profiling a 10 M-row version completes in ≤ 5 min, and its memory **does not scale with row count**: every statistic is aggregated in DuckDB and only aggregates are materialised (see the note below). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-468 | requirement |  | A one-way summary read from a stored Profile returns in < 300 ms (NFR-457); it is never computed on request. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-469 | requirement |  | Validation is deterministic: the same version + rule set version produces byte-identical report bodies apart from timestamps and job ids (FR-11). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-470 | requirement |  | Storage overhead of a Dataset Version is ≤ 1.2× the parquet payload; identical tables across versions are deduplicated by content hash (ID-4). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-471 | requirement |  | The validation report API returns the summary in < 500 ms for reports with up to 500 rules; offending samples are lazily paged. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-472 | requirement |  | Audit: dataset transitions, acknowledgements, dictionary edits, rule-set changes, and purges each emit an Audit Event with before/after state (FR-7). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-473 | requirement |  | A user-supplied `sql` check cannot read outside the target version's parquet files, cannot write, cannot load DuckDB extensions, and is killed at its time budget (FR-48, §4.5). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-474 | requirement |  | Ingestion of a source that fails mid-run leaves no partially-visible version: version rows become visible only on successful commit. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-475 | requirement |  | GLM: 5 M rows × 60 factors converges in < 10 min on a 16-core worker (NFR-456). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-476 | requirement |  | GBM: 5 M rows × 60 factors × 500 trees fits in < 20 min; a custom `expression` objective adds no more than 25 % overhead versus the equivalent builtin. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-477 | requirement |  | Banding/grouping proposals return in < 5 s for a column with ≤ 10 000 distinct levels, computed from the stored Profile where possible. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-478 | requirement |  | A grouping proposal's evidence (FR-107) is computed without summarising the source levels twice. Stated as a requirement rather than left as an implementation detail because it is where NFR-477's budget actually goes: at 10 000 levels a one-way summary costs ~4 s in per-level interval quantiles, and doing it once instead of twice is most of the difference between meeting the budget and missing it. (Added 2026-08-15, WK-661.) |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-479 | requirement |  | Diagnostics computation adds no more than **50 %** to fit wall-clock, at or below **5 M rows × 60 factors**, **excluding the per-factor block that NFR-487 and NFR-488 bound**. *(Amended 2026-08-22, OQ-572 decided — three changes, each because the first measurement contradicted the requirement as written.* ***Scope:*** *FR-172's type-III tests drop each factor and refit, so a diagnostic containing F refits cannot cost 30 % of one fit for any F above zero; that block moves to NFR-487 and the GBM path's per-factor passes to NFR-488.* ***Denominator:*** *fit wall-clock means the elapsed fit call including factor resolution and design-matrix construction — **not** `GlmFitResult.fit_seconds`, which is the solve alone and reads the same arm 1.7× higher, 55.2 % against 32.1 %.* ***Number and scale:*** *30 % was a Phase-0 estimate covering all diagnostics. Re-scoped, the block measures 8.9 %, 9.5 % and 32.1 % across the measured range and projects 34.1 % at 5 M × 60, so the ratio is **not scale-free** — diagnostics are roughly linear in rows while the fit is measured at n^0.837 — and a bare percentage with no stated scale is not a budget. 50 % is 1.47× the worst projection, the headroom this repository carries wherever a budget is met against a measurement at its stated scale (NFR-476 at 1.25×, NFR-485 at 2×, NFR-467 at 3.3×) rather than the 50–2000× residue of its unmeasured Phase-0 guesses.* ***This is not option (d):*** *(d) would have raised the ceiling to accommodate F extra model fits — 1 388 % and climbing with the factor count, which is not a budget. This sets a first ceiling for a quantity that has never carried one, and the 30 % it replaces is preserved above and in OQ-572's options.)* |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-480 | requirement |  | Objective certification completes in < 3 min including the synthetic smoke fit. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-481 | requirement |  | Determinism: identical `spec_hash` + seed reproduces identical coefficients to 1e-10 (GLM) and an identical booster hash (GBM), on the same library versions (FR-11). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-482 | requirement |  | A stored Model round-trips: export → import into a clean instance → predictions identical to the last representable digit (FR-5). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-483 | requirement |  | Security: user-supplied expressions never reach `eval`/`exec`; the parser rejects out-of-grammar input with a position-accurate error; compiled objectives cannot allocate unbounded memory or exceed their per-round time budget (FR-165). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-484 | requirement |  | Audit: factor/banding/grouping creation and edit, fit start and completion, objective derivation/certification/approval, and every status transition emit Audit Events with before/after state. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-485 | requirement |  | Memory: fitting a 5 M × 60 dataset stays within 32 GB, using `QuantileDMatrix`/streaming construction rather than duplicating the design matrix. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-486 | requirement |  | Diagnostics artifacts stay under 50 MB per model; larger evidence (SHAP dependence, residual scatter) goes to content-addressed blobs referenced from the artifact. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-487 | requirement |  | **The GLM per-factor diagnostic block — FR-172's type-III tests — costs no more than one fit wall-clock per *tested* factor.** The unit is one element of `type_iii_tests`, **not** one element of `factors`: interaction operands carry no design column and are skipped (FR-92), a spec with fewer than two factors yields none, and a refit that will not converge is skipped rather than reported. The denominator is a **warm** fit of the same spec at the same scale, for the reason §9's measurement note records. The bound is the mechanism rather than a target: each iteration runs one full `fit_glm` over the remaining factors plus one full `predict_glm`, so a refit of a strictly smaller model that costs more than the fit it is a refit of names a defect rather than a scale. (Added 2026-08-22, OQ-572 decided.) |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-488 | requirement |  | **The GBM per-factor diagnostic block costs no more than 0.06 of one fit per full-population scoring pass.** The unit is a scoring pass, **not** a factor, because the driver is the sum of grid points and not the factor count: partial dependence runs one pass per grid point — ten quantiles for a numeric factor, and **every distinct level for a categorical, uncapped** — so a single 10 000-level column, the scale NFR-477 itself names, costs 10 000 passes that a per-factor budget would record as one factor. Permutation importance is the smaller half by an order of magnitude: at the measured shape it is 61 passes against partial dependence's 560. That the sweep is uncapped, and that it covers every factor where FR-174 says declared ones, is raised as OQ-596 rather than tuned here. (Added 2026-08-22, OQ-572 decided.) |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-489 | requirement |  | Real-time scoring p99 < 50 ms server-side at 200 rps per replica for a ~200-step motor structure with one `exact` GBM call (NFR-454). Without a GBM call, p99 < 15 ms. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-490 | requirement |  | Tracing adds ≤ 20 % to scoring latency and never changes the result (R3). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-491 | requirement |  | A compiled bundle scores with **zero** database or network access; everything it needs is inside it (FR-239). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-492 | requirement |  | Bundle compilation for a large motor structure completes in < 60 s; bundle size stays under 500 MB including booster artifacts. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-493 | requirement |  | Batch scoring ≥ 1 M risks/hour per worker (NFR-455), linear in workers. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-494 | requirement |  | Deployment switchover is atomic with no dropped or mixed-bundle requests, and completes within 30 s of the deploy command including cache warming. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-495 | requirement |  | Determinism: identical bundle hash + quote context ⟹ identical premium, byte-for-byte, across processes, machines, and platform versions (FR-11). |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-496 | requirement |  | Money exactness: no rounding is applied more than once; the ladder reconciles to the penny in 100 % of scored quotes (FR-248), asserted continuously in non-prod and sampled in prod. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-497 | requirement |  | Availability: the scoring endpoint targets 99.95 % monthly, degrading to the last-known-good cached bundle if metadata storage is unavailable. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-498 | requirement |  | Audit: algorithm edits, rate table versions, bulk operations, compilations, approvals, deployments, rollbacks, and routing changes all emit Audit Events with before/after state. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-499 | requirement |  | Security: the scoring API authenticates per Consumer System with scoped credentials and per-client rate limits; quote inputs are never logged in full outside sampled traces, which are access-controlled. *(Clarified 2026-08-30, WK-671: what "logged" reaches, and the store this clause never carved. **The clause governs persistence, not only log output.** The carve-out names sampled traces, and a trace is not a log, so a rule reaching only log lines would have had no need of that exception — the domain is records of a quote input, of which a trace is one. Read that way it collided with FR-260, which has a Golden Quote **store a Quote Context** outside any trace, and the defect is here rather than there: this clause names a single instance where its own justification, *"which are access-controlled"*, states a class. **A full quote input may be held only in an access-controlled artifact this specification names for that purpose, and those are sampled traces (FR-259) and Golden Quotes (FR-260).** A Golden Quote's stored Quote Context carries the same access-control obligation a trace carries, because that property is what justifies the exception rather than the artifact's name. **Any further store requires its own requirement**, so the next one is a visible decision rather than a third silent collision. FR-261 persists a seed rather than quote data and FR-262's sandbox is inline, so neither needs a carve-out. Nothing is in breach: `GoldenQuote` exists in no module, so this is settled before WK-672 builds it. Ruled in `docs/rulings/RL-00917-the-clause-reaches-persistence-and-nfr-499-is-the-defective-one.md` RL-917.)* |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-500 | requirement |  | Trace storage: 1 % sampling of 50 M annual quotes stays under 200 GB/year with the sampled-trace schema. |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-501 | requirement |  | GBM `model_call` steps execute with **`nthread=1` per request**. Measured (S2): single-threading beats all-cores at the tail — p99 1.09 ms vs 1.48 ms, worst case 4.5 ms vs 19.9 ms — because thread-pool spin-up dominates a single-row prediction. Parallelism belongs across concurrent requests, not inside one. *(Amended 2026-08-27, WK-668 — re-measured on the verification machine: p99 1.626 ms vs all-cores' 4.737 ms (max 6.143 ms vs 26.692 ms); `docs/research/w8-spike-resolution.md`. nthread=1 stays 0.34x of all-cores at the tail — the original S2 order holds, though the absolute figures are higher on this machine (slower `DMatrix` construction). p99 1.626 ms is still 3.3 % of the 50 ms budget: PASS. The design rule is unchanged: `nthread=1` per request.)* |  |  |  |  |
| NFR-502 | requirement |  | The scoring endpoint does **not** apply `response_model` validation to its response. Pydantic validation costs roughly 1 ms per request — 2 % of the 50 ms budget before any pricing work — and the response path otherwise runs three to five transformations. `ScoringResult` is constructed by `pricing-core` and is already trusted, so it is serialised directly with a C-speed encoder (`ORJSONResponse`). Inbound `QuoteContext` **is** validated: untrusted input must be checked, trusted output need not be. *(Amended 2026-08-27, WK-668 — the premise's ~1 ms figure was not reproduced. A realistic `ScoringResult` (premium, 20 rate steps, 60 factors, metadata) validates and serialises at p99 0.070 ms, 0.14 % of the 50 ms budget, on the verification machine; `docs/research/w8-spike-resolution.md`. The measured shape is the one the premise describes, so the figure was an over-estimate, not a different context. The design rule is unchanged: validate inbound, never outbound; encode with `ORJSONResponse`.)* *(Amended 2026-08-29, WK-671 Slice 2 — the rule now states the property and no longer names the class. **Validate inbound, never outbound; serialise the trusted result directly with a compiled encoder.** `ORJSONResponse` was named when it was the way to get one. It is deprecated in the pinned FastAPI (0.141.1), it asserts at **render** rather than at import when `orjson` is absent — so a lost dependency boots clean and fails on the first quote — and the replacement its own deprecation notice names, a return type or `response_model`, is outbound validation, which this requirement's first sentence forbids: measured on the verification machine, an annotated route returning a shape that violates its model answers 500, and one returning a valid model drops any extra key. Pydantic v2's own compiled serialiser satisfies the property with no new dependency — `model_dump_json` emits an unvalidated model's contents verbatim, and a raw `Response` carrying those bytes runs no outbound validation at all. Ruled in `docs/rulings/RL-00883-f1-nfr-502-is-amended-to-the-property-it-was-always-about-orjson-is-not-added.md` RL-883.)* |  |  |  |  |

| NFR-535 | requirement |  | **A Model scores in a process where the libraries that *fit* models cannot be imported — proven by scoring in a subprocess with `glum`, `scikit-learn`, `celery` and `dagster` made unimportable, not by review.** (OQ-642, decided 2026-08-18.) One image with a role flag ships through Phases 1–2 and a separate scoring image from Phase 3, and the only thing that can make that split cheap later is that the scoring path never grew a fitting dependency in between. NFR-534 states the runtime property against the *worker pool*; this states it against the *dependency set*, which is what a second image would actually have to shed. `xgboost` and `lightgbm` are deliberately **not** on the forbidden side: `02` FR-193 scores a GBM by loading its JSON booster, so a boosting library is a scoring dependency by design — what the split sheds is the libraries that fit. **An import-linter contract is the wrong instrument here, and was tried first.** `glum` and `sklearn` are already imported at their call sites rather than at module scope — inside `fit_glm`, `propose_banding` and `propose_grouping` — which is exactly the discipline this requirement wants; import-linter reads the AST and counts a function-scope import like any other, so a contract over `pricing_core.modelling.predict` reports four violations against code that is already correct, and the only ways to green it are to weaken it or to move modules that have no other reason to move. The property is a runtime one, so the check creates the runtime. The slice that builds the scoring API (Phase 2) extends the same test to that entry point rather than adding a second mechanism. |  |  |  |  |

| OQ-538 | open question |  | ~~Burning cost — claim amount divided by exposure — is classified **two opposite ways in the same repository**, and FR-10's boundary (OQ-539, decided above) forbids both standings at once. `Reconciliation.observed_burning_cost_minor` and `ReconciledPeril.modelled_burning_cost_minor` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/perils.py:290,306-307`) are strict `MoneyMinor` integers carrying the `..._minor` suffix; `OneWayRow.mean_burning_cost` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/profiles.py:162`) is `float \| None` with no suffix, and that class's own docstring (`:145-148`) states the rule as *"they are statistics, not amounts"*. Same quantity, same arithmetic shape, opposite verdicts. FR-10 now tests **what a quantity denominates**, and what these denominate is identical, so the divergence is no longer expressible.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): (b) — burning cost is a statistic, with the integer kept.** Claim amount per exposure-year is a rate, and FR-10's test is what a quantity denominates — identical on both sides of the divergence, as the computing code states outright: *"Burning cost is an exposure-weighted mean, not a portfolio total"* (module docstring, `packages/pricing-core/src/pricing_core/modelling/perils.py`), where both figures divide by `exposure_total` (`:192`, `:212`, `:217`). The row's bare `perils.py` computation cites belong to that file, not model-schema's. OQ-544's decided basis — a mean per unit is a statistic, specified as `01` FR-64 — reaches the reconciliation figures by precedent, so (c)'s distinguishing predicate is carried by no artifact, and (a) is refuted by FR-64's stated precision rationale. **The suffix is the defect, not the type**: FR-23 is unscoped, so the `..._minor` names must go; FR-10 only *permits* a float for a statistic, and the exact-equality parts-sum check (`model-schema/perils.py:364-367`, built at `pricing-core:224`) is the reason to keep the integer. **Released work (mechanical):** rename `observed_burning_cost_minor` and `modelled_burning_cost_minor` in `model-schema/perils.py:290,306-307` and `pricing-core/modelling/perils.py:85,93-94`, types unchanged; consumers are the two perils test suites, and the generated contracts follow. | **(a) Burning cost is an amount.** `mean_burning_cost` becomes `MoneyMinor` and gains the suffix. Costs the confidence-interval precision `OneWayRow`'s docstring says the float exists to preserve, and FR-64 is cited there for it. **(b) Burning cost is a statistic** — a rate of money per exposure-year, money only once multiplied back by exposure. `observed_/modelled_burning_cost_minor` lose the suffix under FR-23; whether they also lose the integer type is separable, since FR-10 *permits* a float for a statistic rather than requiring one, and `perils.py:221-224`'s sum-of-the-rounded-parts invariant is a real reason to keep the integer. **(c) They are different quantities** and the divergence is correct — requires stating the distinguishing predicate, which no artifact currently carries. |  |  |  |
| OQ-538 | open question |  | ~~Burning cost — claim amount divided by exposure — is classified **two opposite ways in the same repository**, and FR-10's boundary (OQ-539, decided above) forbids both standings at once. `Reconciliation.observed_burning_cost_minor` and `ReconciledPeril.modelled_burning_cost_minor` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/perils.py:290,306-307`) are strict `MoneyMinor` integers carrying the `..._minor` suffix; `OneWayRow.mean_burning_cost` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/profiles.py:162`) is `float \| None` with no suffix, and that class's own docstring (`:145-148`) states the rule as *"they are statistics, not amounts"*. Same quantity, same arithmetic shape, opposite verdicts. FR-10 now tests **what a quantity denominates**, and what these denominate is identical, so the divergence is no longer expressible.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): (b) — burning cost is a statistic, the integer kept; the `..._minor` suffix is the defect FR-23 governs and the rename is released work.** Money per exposure-year is a rate: the computing code states *"an exposure-weighted mean, not a portfolio total"*, and OQ-544's decided basis (a mean per unit is a statistic, FR-64) reaches the reconciliation figures by precedent — the full record is in open-questions.md. *Recommendation: (b), burning cost is a statistic, with the integer kept — the `..._minor` suffix is the defect FR-23 governs, not the type FR-10 only permits. Open; filed by the change that made it visible rather than decided inside it.* **Two objections this row has already drawn, answered here so they are not re-run.** *That they are different quantities — a per-level mean against a part-level total.* The perils figure is not a total: `perils.py:192` computes `_to_minor(float(np.sum(observed)) / exposure_total)`, a weighted mean per exposure-year across the part, and `Reconciliation.ratio` (`:337-338`) divides modelled by observed so the exposure denominators cancel — which is a thing that works because both sides are rates. The two therefore differ in **grouping**, part against level, not in kind. *That `OneWayRow`'s float is deliberate rather than an oversight.* It is, and the row does not claim otherwise: the docstring states the reason and cites FR-64 for it, which is why option (a) is the expensive one. A stated reason on one side of a divergence settles that side, not the divergence. **The naming half is FR-23's territory and the typing half FR-10's**; this row is filed against the pair because the recommendation splits between them. **One side of this has a decision of record, found after filing.** OQ-544 — maintainer-accepted 2026-08-17 and specified as `01` FR-64 — decided that `01` FR-61's one-way row renames `severity_minor` and `burning_cost_minor`, *"which are **means** and therefore floats"*, to `mean_severity` and `mean_burning_cost`. So the `profiles.py` side is not an unresolved standing but a decided one, and its basis — a mean per unit is a statistic — reaches `perils.py`'s figures too, since `:192` and `:217` both divide by `exposure_total`. That makes **`perils.py` the divergent side**, and narrows this row from *which of the two changes* to *whether an aggregate reconciliation figure is exempt from the basis OQ-544 already decided on*. Recommendation (b) is unchanged and now rests on a precedent rather than only an argument. Raised by `w6b-planner` as evidence rather than as a verdict, after it had been wrong twice on this row. *Renamed 2026-08-26 (W6b-15): `observed_burning_cost_minor` and `modelled_burning_cost_minor` become `observed_burning_cost` and `modelled_burning_cost`, types unchanged. The names cited above are the pre-rename names, kept as the record.* |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-539 | open question |  | ~~**What governs a monetary value carried inside a `dict[str, Any]` that the generated contract publishes as an unconstrained object, and how far does FR-10's guarantee reach?** `RuleResult.measured` and `RuleResult.threshold` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/validation.py:455`) carry money-bearing entries into every published validation report, and `docs/contracts/schemas/generated/validation-report.schema.json` declares both as an object with `additionalProperties` true — the contract asserts nothing about what is inside them.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-24 — option (c), write the boundary down: FR-10 now states it.** The carve-out reaches statistics, not amounts, and the test is **what a quantity denominates, not how it was computed**; money stays integer minor units or `Decimal` *wherever it appears*, including inside an untyped `dict[str, Any]`, which is what closes this row's actual gap — a boundary scoped to *declared* fields would have left the untyped payload exactly where it was. *Consequences:* option (a), typing the payload, becomes the mechanism that lets the existing checks see the value rather than the thing that creates the obligation, and is now specified rather than guessed at; it is **not** wholly mechanical, because `threshold_minor`'s quantile branch needs a rounding direction that moves the check's own verdict. ~~`audit-docs.py` check 12's appeal to FR-10 for a `_minor` naming rule FR-10 does not state is **not** fixed here and must not be read as fixed~~ — FR-23 records it. *(Second half struck 2026-08-24.)* *Not fixed here* was true of this change; **must not be read as fixed** was already false when written. `d2a4773` (#181) had repaired the citation and is an ancestor of `e527984`, the commit that wrote the warning. Standing rather than scoped, it invites exactly the edit FR-23 exists to prevent — re-opening a closed repair, or correcting the citation a second time in the wrong direction. It is correct at `scripts/audit-docs.py:547-550` and the failure message emits `(FR-23)` at `:556`. Found by `w6b-auditor` while verifying an exoneration it was itself the beneficiary of. | Raised 2026-08-24 (W6b-13b) while checking whether that slice's catalogue defaults were safe to publish. **The purpose-built check already exists, already runs on this exact document, and still cannot see the value.** `test_generated_and_authored_agree_on_scalar_types` (`backend/tests/test_contracts.py:1013`, marked for FR-64) was added in response to `01` FR-64's second propagation failure — its docstring names the 45812.42 case verbatim — and `validation-report` is in `COMPARED_SLUGS`. Its stated scope is the gap: *“Only paths present on both sides are compared.”* A `dict[str, Any]` published with `additionalProperties` true has no declared path on either side, so the remedy the repository adopted after that failure is **field-shaped**, and this value is not a field. Two further instruments are blind on the same path: `test_no_generated_money_field_admits_a_json_number` (`backend/tests/test_contracts.py:136`) walks generated JSON Schema *property names*, so runtime dict keys are unreachable by construction; and `audit-docs.py`'s check 12 reads markdown and schema files only, matching a `_minor` key against a fractional literal, which no Python source and no runtime payload can present to it. The carrier itself is untyped: `CheckOutcome` (`packages/pricing-core/src/pricing_core/data/validate.py:55`) is a plain frozen dataclass, not Pydantic and not from `model-schema`, so `MoneyMinor` never reaches it. The control sits on the same model — `affected_exposure_fraction` *is* a named, typed property and *is* within the money scan's reach. **The name-shape guard is not the problem:** its own comment says it recognises a *shape* of name “so the next ratio needs no entry and no decision”, deliberately a rule rather than the hand-maintained list OQ-544 objected to; give the payload a declared shape and that guard arms itself unchanged. **The second half is where FR-10 stops.** Its positive rule is scoped to “the rating path and all persisted rate tables”, and a validation report is neither, while its second sentence *permits* floats “inside model fitting and diagnostics” — so `threshold_minor` and `largest_minor` published as floats (`validate.py:957`, `:971`, `:978`, `:982`) breach no requirement textually. Yet the platform does not behave as though the carve-out reaches amounts: `OneWayRow` (`packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/profiles.py:141`) is itself a diagnostic artifact and still types `claim_amount_minor` as strict `MoneyMinor`, and `validate.py:918` casts with `int(...)`. That reading — the carve-out covers *statistics*, not *amounts* — is written nowhere. Options: **(a) type the payload**, a declared shape per check family or `MoneyMinor` on the entries that really are amounts, after which both existing checks reach it and no new rule is written; **(b) extend the money scan to dict keys**, cheap, but it must infer which runtime keys are money from names alone, which is the inference that fails below; **(c) write the boundary down only**, amending FR-10 to say whether a diagnostic payload is in scope and accepting that nothing enforces it. |  |  |  |
| OQ-539 | open question |  | ~~What governs a monetary value carried inside a `dict[str, Any]` that the generated contract publishes as an unconstrained object, and how far does FR-10's guarantee reach? `RuleResult.measured` and `RuleResult.threshold` reach the published validation report with `additionalProperties` true, so the purpose-built type check (`test_generated_and_authored_agree_on_scalar_types`, which *does* compare `validation-report`) cannot see them — it compares only paths present on both sides — the money scan walks property names, so dict keys are unreachable, and `audit-docs.py` check 12 reads only markdown and schemas. FR-10's positive rule is scoped to the rating path and rate tables and permits floats in diagnostics, so nothing is textually breached; yet `OneWayRow`, itself a diagnostic, types its amount strictly. Options: type the payload; extend the scan to dict keys; or write the boundary down only. Recommendation: type the payload, and amend FR-10's scope alongside it.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-24 — option (c), write the boundary down: FR-10 now states it.** The carve-out reaches statistics, not amounts, and the test is **what a quantity denominates, not how it was computed**; money stays integer minor units or `Decimal` *wherever it appears*, including inside an untyped `dict[str, Any]`, which is what closes this row's actual gap — a boundary scoped to *declared* fields would have left the untyped payload exactly where it was. *Consequences:* option (a), typing the payload, becomes the mechanism that lets the existing checks see the value rather than the thing that creates the obligation, and is now specified rather than guessed at; it is **not** wholly mechanical, because `threshold_minor`'s quantile branch needs a rounding direction that moves the check's own verdict. ~~`audit-docs.py` check 12's appeal to FR-10 for a `_minor` naming rule FR-10 does not state is **not** fixed here and must not be read as fixed~~ — FR-23 records it. *(Second half struck 2026-08-24.)* *Not fixed here* was true of this change; **must not be read as fixed** was already false when written. `d2a4773` (#181) had repaired the citation and is an ancestor of `e527984`, the commit that wrote the warning. Standing rather than scoped, it invites exactly the edit FR-23 exists to prevent — re-opening a closed repair, or correcting the citation a second time in the wrong direction. It is correct at `scripts/audit-docs.py:547-550` and the failure message emits `(FR-23)` at `:556`. Found by `w6b-auditor` while verifying an exoneration it was itself the beneficiary of. **This decision releases work explicitly deferred onto it.** `d2a4773` recorded three `..._minor` names published on floats by `_large_loss` and `_severity_range` (`packages/pricing-core/src/pricing_core/data/validate.py`) and scheduled their correction *with this row* rather than inline, because they are rendered verbatim as user-visible labels by `RuleResultRow.vue`. The boundary above settles all three, and **it settles them in two opposite directions** — so a sweep treating them as one population corrects two of them wrongly. `mean_severity_minor` (`:1077`) denominates a statistic, already settled as one by OQ-544 on 2026-08-17: the float is correct and **the name is the defect**, so it is renamed and its type left alone. `threshold_minor` and `largest_minor` (`:971,978,982`) denominate money — a claim-amount cut and a largest claim amount — so **the name is correct and the type is the defect**, and renaming them would destroy the marker while leaving the float. The fix inverts between the two groups, and the `..._minor` suffix cannot tell them apart because it is the thing under test in one group and the evidence in the other. *(Population corrected 2026-08-24 — it was under-wide by two.)* **The enumeration above is three names; the population is five.** `min_severity_minor` and `max_severity_minor` (`:1072-1073`, echoed at `:1078`) belong in the rename group, and **FR-23 says so twelve rows above this one** — it cites `:1072`–`:1073` alongside `:1077` and `:1078` as `validate.py` publishing the suffix *"on a mean severity, a ratio"*. This row and its own requirement therefore disagreed while sitting in the same document. The corollary landed in `da7ba2c` settles it independently: both **bound `severity`**, the mean severity at `:1073`, and a bound takes its kind from the quantity it bounds — so they are a statistic's bounds, their float is **permitted rather than a defect**, and the suffix is the defect. Sorted by dimension rather than by whether name and type happen to agree — **rename and keep the float** (bounds on, or measures of, a statistic): `min_severity_minor`, `max_severity_minor`, `mean_severity_minor`; **retype to integer and keep the name** (amounts): `threshold_minor`, `largest_minor`. Every field bounding or measuring mean severity now moves together, where the earlier split separated a measure from the two bounds that constrain it. **Why it was missed is the same trap from the opposite side**: at `:1072-1073` the name and the type agreed with *each other* — a `_minor` suffix on a value the rule catalogue writes as an integer — and agreement between name and type reads as conformance, when the question is whether either matches the **quantity**. Retracted by `w6b-decision-maker`, which had filed the original disposition. **One further surface is a name to neither rule and so is governed by neither**: `detail=f"mean severity is {severity:,.0f} minor units"` (`:1079`) asserts minor units on the same statistic in a user-visible string. FR-23 reserves *names* and check 12 matches names in markdown and schema files, so nothing reaches it — renaming the three fields and leaving this string still ships a payload telling a reader that mean severity is in minor units. *Renamed 2026-08-26 (W6b-15): `min_severity_minor`, `max_severity_minor` and `mean_severity_minor` become `min_severity`, `max_severity` and `mean_severity`. `threshold_minor` and `largest_minor` keep their names. Their float-to-int correction stays released and unscheduled. The names cited above are the pre-rename names, kept as the record.* |  |  |  |  |

| OQ-543 | open question |  | ~~How are the five workflow journeys (`docs/workflows/WF-698…05`) evidenced?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-15: the mechanical citation audit now, one end-to-end test per journey as its last module lands, never a marker on an existing test.** | Found by plan review 2: **no test in the repository cites a journey**, and `audit-docs.py`'s "workflow coverage" measures whether a workflow document *mentions* a requirement id, not whether the journey runs. Options: **(a)** mark existing tests with the journey id — cheapest, and the weakest, since a marker on a slice claims the whole; **(b)** one end-to-end journey test per workflow, written when its modules exist — real evidence, but WF-698's modelling half cannot be written until WK-661; **(c)** a mechanical audit that every step in a journey cites an endpoint, requirement or artifact that exists — catches drift, proves nothing about execution. |  |  |  |
| OQ-543 | open question |  | ~~How are the five workflow journeys (`workflows/WF-698…05`) evidenced?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-15: a mechanical citation audit now, one end-to-end test per journey as its last module lands, and never a marker on an existing test.** Specified as FR-19. Raised by plan review 2, 2026-08-15, when the "workflow coverage" figure turned out to measure whether a journey *mentions* a requirement id. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-544 | open question |  | ~~Should `01` FR-61's one-way row rename `severity_minor` and `burning_cost_minor`, which are **means** and therefore floats, not integer minor units?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-17: rename to `mean_severity` and `mean_burning_cost`, in the slice that next changes the profile contract.** | Raised 2026-08-15 (WK-661) by generating `banding` and `grouping` schemas: they embed the one-way row, and the money scan flagged both fields the moment it could see them. `01` keeps them as floats deliberately — rounding a mean to whole minor units would lose the precision the confidence interval beside it expresses — so the values are right and the **names** are what FR-10 objects to, because `_minor` is reserved for integer minor units. **Rename** (e.g. `mean_severity`, `mean_burning_cost`, both in units of the workspace currency's minor unit): the scan needs no exclusion and a reader stops having to know which `_minor` fields are exceptions — at the cost of a breaking change to `01`'s published profile contract, the frontend that reads it, and the seeded demo. **Keep and exclude**: nothing breaks, and the repository carries a money-discipline check with a hand-maintained allow-list, which is the shape of thing that grows. |  |  |  |
| OQ-544 | open question |  | ~~Should `01` FR-61's one-way row rename `severity_minor` and `burning_cost_minor`? They are means, and therefore floats, while `_minor` is reserved for integer minor units (FR-10).~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-17: rename to `mean_severity` and `mean_burning_cost`, in the slice that next changes the profile contract — not as a change of its own, and not never.** Specified as `01` FR-64, which carries the trigger and the owner. ~~Until then the two names are excluded by name from the money scan, and the exclusion may not grow: a money-discipline allow-list is exactly the kind of exception nobody revisits.~~ **Corrected 2026-08-24: delivered 2026-08-18.** The exclusion was deleted rather than grown, and post-rename `mean_severity` carries no `_minor` for the money scan to exclude, so the condition above was discharged rather than breached. See **OQ-539** for the names in `pricing-core`'s `validate.py` that the rename never reached. Raised 2026-08-15 by generating the `banding` and `grouping` schemas, which embed the row. |  |  |  |  |

| OQ-547 | open question |  | ~~`DecimalStr` is the repository's exact-decimal type, and it silently accepts a `float`. `OneWayRow(exposure_years=0.1+0.2)` returns `Decimal('0.30000000000000004')` — the float's binary error preserved verbatim inside a field FR-10 defines as exact. Should `DecimalStr` refuse a `float` outright?~~ | Raised 2026-08-19 (WK-661, the `top_levels` slice), from an implementer's question rather than a spec review. **11 `DecimalStr` fields across 6 modules** are affected. *(Corrected 2026-08-19 from "26 across 7": that figure counted every line mentioning `DecimalStr` — imports, `__all__`, and `money.py`'s own definition — of which there are exactly 26. The audit the decision required is what found it.)* The new `LevelCount.exposure_years` carries a field-scoped validator that does refuse floats, making it the only strict one — a deliberate inconsistency recorded here rather than generalised without a decision. Serialisation is not the hole: `DecimalStr` emits a JSON **string** and declares itself one in JSON Schema, so every round-trip through the contracts is already safe. The exposure is a caller constructing a model in Python, or a hand-written client posting a JSON number. |  |  |  |
| OQ-547 | open question |  | ~~`DecimalStr` silently accepts a `float`: `OneWayRow(exposure_years=0.1+0.2)` yields `Decimal('0.30000000000000004')`, binary error preserved inside a field FR-10 calls exact. Should it refuse floats outright?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-19: yes — refuse at validation, and put the rule on the type rather than on the field.** Specified as FR-21 and delivered the same day. The audit the decision turned on found **no caller passing a float**: the one path that computes exposure in float already quantised at the boundary (`_stored_exposure`), so the change cost nothing to adopt and the hole was open only to callers not yet written. `LevelCount.exposure_years`'s hand-written validator is deleted, not duplicated — 11 fields across 6 modules now get the guarantee one field had — the question said 26 across 7, which counted every line mentioning `DecimalStr` rather than every field using it. Raised 2026-08-19 (WK-661). |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-548 | open question |  | ~~Nothing in this repository cross-checks a spec's declared error codes (each module's §5.1 "Error codes" table) against `backend/src/app/errors.py`, the registry that makes a code raisable. Should one exist, and if so where?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-21: yes — folded into `audit-docs.py`'s existing error-code pass.** Specified as FR-22. | Verified twice on the FR-137 branch: `02` §5.1 declares `MODEL_APPROXIMATION_INVALID`, `errors.py` registers it in `MODELLING_ERROR_CODES`, and no test, script or audit compares the two lists. `test_errors.py::test_spec_error_codes_are_all_constructible` iterates only `PLATFORM_ERROR_CODES`; `audit-docs.py`'s error-code check (`scripts/audit-docs.py`, ownership check) tests exclusivity **between spec documents** and never opens `errors.py`; `scope-audit.py` has no error-code check at all. The one related test, `packages/pricing-core/tests/test_spec_hash.py::test_every_code_the_fit_path_can_raise_is_registered`, walks one `pricing-core` file's AST for raise sites — code-vs-registry, not spec-vs-registry. **The gap is structural and applies to every module**, not only `02`; `07` gets incidental coverage only because a test happens to name it. **(a)** a script (`scripts/`, following `scope-audit.py`'s shape) that parses each spec's §5.1 error-code table and diffs it against `errors.py`'s `*_ERROR_CODES` frozensets, run in the gate — cheap, mechanical, and it is the same shape as the import-linter contract that was dead for a day and reported success throughout (`CLAUDE.md` §13.4), so it must itself be proven to fail on a deliberately introduced divergence. **(b)** fold the check into `audit-docs.py`'s existing error-code pass, since that already parses every spec's error-code table for the ownership check and only needs a second comparison added. **(c)** a test in `backend/tests/test_errors.py`, parametrised over every module's error-code frozenset the way `test_spec_error_codes_are_all_constructible` already is for `PLATFORM_ERROR_CODES`, extended to read each spec file rather than trusting the frozenset alone. |  |  |  |
| OQ-548 | open question |  | ~~Nothing cross-checks a spec's declared error codes (each module's §5.1 table) against `backend/src/app/errors.py`, the registry that makes a code raisable — verified on the FR-137 branch that `02` §5.1's `MODEL_APPROXIMATION_INVALID` is registered while no test, script or audit compares the two lists at all; `audit-docs.py`'s error-code check only tests exclusivity between spec documents. The gap is structural and applies to every module. Should a check exist, and where — a standalone script, folded into `audit-docs.py`'s existing error-code pass, or a parametrised test in `backend/tests/test_errors.py`?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-21: fold the comparison into `audit-docs.py`'s existing error-code pass — FR-22**, owner the maintainer, before Phase 1a's exit demo. Raised 2026-08-19 (WK-661, the GLM-approximation-as-a-Model slice). |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-549 | open question |  | ~~What is a Contents cell in each module spec's §5.3 Frontend views table — an enumeration of what the view must show, or a prose summary of its character — and how does a cell declare which kind it is? Raised 2026-08-24 (WK-664) by `02` §5.3's Diagnostics cell, which a filed slice map read as an enumeration and turned into a chart count that then bound a slice.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-24: a §5.3 Contents cell is prose and binds nothing, with a declared-exhaustive and a declared-prose affordance — FR-24.** In force now, with a **per-cell** carve-out rather than a suspensive one: it binds every cell except the seven carrying an obligation held in no requirement and no contract, each of which stays binding until discharged, because ruling them non-binding retroactively would repeal all seven with no record. *(Clarified 2026-08-24: the original wording read as suspensive — that the whole rule was inert until all seven discharged — which is not what was decided and would have made both slices below unplannable.)* *Consequences:* `W6b-1b` becomes plannable on the rule; `W6b-9` additionally waits on `02` §5.3 Diagnostics being declared prose, which is discharged in this change; `W6b-1a` was never affected. | **The table is not consistent with itself, and nothing marks which kind a cell is.** In `02` §5.3 alone: Model detail and Diagnostics are category-noun summaries; the Objective certificate row is an exhaustive enumeration of all four `CheckStatus` values with a presentation rule attached; the Peril structure library row enumerates its *negative* space, recording that there is **no usage count** and that its absence is by specification rather than pending. Three kinds of cell, one table, no marker. **The Diagnostics cell was wrong in both directions at once**: it still named double-lift a week after FR-171 struck it on 2026-08-17, and it named none of `partial_dependence`, `permutation_importances`, `monotonicity`, `quantile_crossing`, `type_iii_tests`, `vif`, `aliasing` or `leverage_blob`, all of which the generated contract carries. So a reader who trusts the cell can both under-scope a view and re-litigate a decision already written down, and the two failures are opposite. **The problem is cross-cutting rather than local**: `00` §5.6, the canonical view inventory, registers 7 of `02` §5.3's 12 views, omits 5 and carries 1 name with no §5.3 row — and its model row names three views while giving two routes, Diagnostics having none, where every other slash-separated row in that table gives one route per view: `Dataset list / detail / version` gives three routes for three views and `Peril structure library / detail` gives two for two. The model row is not the only one short of route-per-view, which cuts toward this question rather than against it: `Quote sandbox & trace viewer` names two views on one route, so the table has no route-per-view rule for the model row to be anomalous against. **Options.** *(a)* every Contents cell is prose and the generated contract is the only enumeration. *(b)* every cell is exhaustive and a view may show nothing not written in it, so the cell is amended first. *(c)* a cell declares its own kind in the cell, which the Peril structure row already does in prose and could be made the rule. |  |  |  |
| OQ-549 | open question |  | ~~What is a Contents cell in each module spec's §5.3 Frontend views table — an enumeration of what the view must show, or a prose summary of its character — and how does a cell declare which kind it is? Raised 2026-08-24 (WK-664): a filed slice map read `02` §5.3's Diagnostics cell as an enumeration and derived from it a chart count that then bound a slice. The cell was wrong in both directions — it still named double-lift a week after FR-171 struck it, and it named none of the further fields `GbmDiagnostics` and `GlmDiagnostics` carry in the generated contract. Cells in the same table do three different things: the Model detail and Diagnostics rows are category-noun summaries, the Objective certificate row enumerates all four `CheckStatus` values with a presentation rule, and the Peril structure library row enumerates its *negative* space. Nothing distinguishes them. Mirrored in `docs/open-questions.md`.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-24: a §5.3 Contents cell is prose and binds nothing, with a declared-exhaustive and a declared-prose affordance — FR-24.** In force now, with a **per-cell** carve-out rather than a suspensive one: it binds every cell except the seven carrying an obligation held in no requirement and no contract, each of which stays binding until discharged, because ruling them non-binding retroactively would repeal all seven with no record. *(Clarified 2026-08-24: the original wording read as suspensive — that the whole rule was inert until all seven discharged — which is not what was decided and would have made both slices below unplannable.)* *Consequences:* `W6b-1b` becomes plannable on the rule; `W6b-9` additionally waits on `02` §5.3 Diagnostics being declared prose, which is discharged in this change; `W6b-1a` was never affected. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-550 | open question |  | ~~Should `ChartFigure` relate a row's values to its column headers by **name** rather than by position? The component takes `columns: readonly string[]` and `rows: readonly (readonly (string \| number \| null)[])[]` as two independent props, and "in the same order as `columns`" is a docstring. Every caller reaches it by hand-transcribing an already-correct ECharts option into that pair, which is the one activity that produces a value published under the wrong heading — a table that says something different from the chart beside it, which is the specific failure NFR-463 exists to prevent.~~ | **(a) Positional rows, with the two checks W6b-9 built** — a dev-only arity guard in `ChartFigure` and `cellUnder(table, row, column)` in `frontend/src/test-tables.ts`, which reads a cell by the header above it. The two catch disjoint classes (a wrong *number* of cells at runtime; a wrong *alignment* under test) and both are proven on deliberately broken input. Neither makes a misaligned table impossible: the correspondence remains a convention each call site re-honours, and the helper only fires where a test was written to use it. **(b) Rows keyed by column name** — `Record<string, string \| number \| null>` per row, keys constrained to `columns` by the type system. Misalignment becomes a compile error and the ordering question stops existing; costs a rewrite of every call site and fixture, and turns display strings into identifiers, so renaming a heading becomes a code change. **(c) A column descriptor** — `{ name, value: (row: T) => Cell }` with rows staying the caller's own domain objects, which is where most table libraries land. Strongest and the only option that removes the transcription step itself, but it makes `ChartFigure` generic and is the largest change of the three. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: deferral confirmed — (b) or (c) at the next slice that adds a ChartFigure caller; the 2026-08-25 record stands** |  |  |  |
| OQ-550 | open question |  | ~~Should `ChartFigure` relate a row's values to its column headers by~~ **name** rather than by position? `columns` and `rows` are independent props and their correspondence is a docstring, so a hand-transcribed call site can publish a value under the wrong heading — a table saying something different from the chart beside it, which is what NFR-463 exists to prevent. W6b-9 shipped the two checks that catch the arity error at runtime and the alignment error under test; keyed rows or a column descriptor would make the error unrepresentable instead. **Deferred** with an owner (CLAUDE.md §13) 2026-08-25 to the next slice that adds a chart, because the cost of the positional shape is per-call-site and grows with each one. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: deferral confirmed — (b) or (c) at the next slice that adds a ChartFigure caller; the 2026-08-25 record stands** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-551 | open question |  | ~~Where does a view get the **workspace currency** it formats minor-unit amounts with? `ProfileView.vue:22` declares `currency?: string` and `:40` resolves `props.currency ?? "GBP"`; `VersionDetailView.vue:15,25` does the same. Both are mounted only by `router/index.ts:37,43` with `props: true`, which supplies route *params* — `slug` and `version` — so `currency` is never passed by anything and the `"GBP"` fallback is not a fallback but the only path. `formatMinor(row.claim_amount_minor, currency)` therefore prints `£` over a euro-denominated dataset. The defect is invisible because the frontend's hardcoded `"GBP"` matches `Dataset.currency`'s own `@default GBP` in the generated contract, so every dataset that has not changed it renders correctly and the one that has renders wrongly with no warning. The right value exists and is simply not threaded: `Dataset.currency` is a required `string` on the `Dataset` schema. It is **not** on `DatasetVersion`, which is what these views already fetch, so no option here is free. Raised 2026-08-25 (W6b-9), which made `OneWayChart`'s new `currency` prop **required** so the retrofit could not propagate the default further, but did not fix the views — a money defect landing inside an accessibility commit is reviewed by someone looking at table markup. Mirrored in `docs/specs/00-overview.md` §10.~~ | **(a) Thread it through the route** — resolve the dataset before navigation and pass `currency` as a prop. Keeps the views pure functions of their props, which is why `props: true` was chosen, and keeps the money value on the same path as `slug`. Costs a router-level fetch or a navigation guard, and every future view showing an amount must be added to it. **(b) Read it in the view** — call `getDataset(slug)`, which already exists and returns `Dataset`, alongside the existing `getVersion`. Local, needs no router change, and each view that needs currency asks for it. Costs one extra request per page and repeats the fetch in every such view. **(c) Hold it in a store** — a Pinia store keyed by slug, fetched once. Cheapest at the third view and the natural home for a value that is per-dataset and read everywhere; the most machinery to add for the two views that need it today. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — views read the dataset's currency via getDataset(slug); the ?? "GBP" fallbacks are deleted** |  |  |  |
| OQ-551 | open question |  | ~~Where does a view get the~~ **workspace currency** it formats minor-unit amounts with? `ProfileView.vue` and `VersionDetailView.vue` both declare `currency?: string` and resolve `props.currency ?? "GBP"`, but both are mounted only by `router/index.ts` with `props: true`, which supplies route *params* — `slug` and `version` — so nothing ever passes `currency` and the fallback is the only path. `formatMinor(row.claim_amount_minor, currency)` therefore prints `£` over a euro-denominated dataset. It is invisible because the hardcoded `"GBP"` matches `Dataset.currency`'s own `@default GBP` in the generated contract: every dataset that has not changed it renders correctly, and the one that has renders wrongly with no warning (FR-10). The value exists on `Dataset` and not on `DatasetVersion`, which is what these views fetch, so the fix is either a threaded prop, a second `getDataset(slug)` call, or a per-dataset store — no option is free. Raised 2026-08-25 (W6b-9), which made `OneWayChart`'s new `currency` prop **required** so the retrofit could not propagate the default further, but did not fix the views. *(Amended 2026-08-25: a third view — the factor workbench — has **no route** to the currency, not merely an unthreaded prop: it holds a dataset *version* id, `DatasetVersion` carries no currency, and `/datasets/{dataset_id}` is PATCH-only. So option (b), fetching by slug, does not reach it, and a route-level fix — a `currency` on `DatasetVersion`, or a GET by id — enters the option set. `W6b-5b` omits the incurred column rather than guess.)* **Open**, owned by the maintainer as a money-correctness decision; options, trade-offs and a recommendation are in `docs/open-questions.md`. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — views read the dataset's currency via getDataset(slug); the ?? "GBP" fallbacks are deleted** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-552 | open question |  | ~~How does the SPA render a **principal it did not authenticate as**? W6b-3 added `01` §5.3's owner column and could only render `owner_id` itself — a bare uuid. FR-82 says the column displays `owner_id`, and justifies the field on the ground that `06`'s RBAC and approval trails "need a named subject — 'who owns this data' is a question a workspace cannot answer". A uuid does not name a subject either, so the column satisfies the requirement's sentence while defeating the sentence that motivates it. Nothing in the platform can close the gap: there is **no `/api/v1/users`** and no principal-lookup route of any kind in the published contract; the only identity endpoint is `/api/v1/me`, whose `Me` schema carries `principal_id` and `display` **for the caller alone**. There is also no convention to follow — `frontend/src/api/me.ts` does not exist, `frontend/src/stores/` is still empty, and before W6b-3 no view in the SPA rendered a principal, actor or owner id anywhere. This is therefore not a formatting choice but the platform's **first identity rendering**, which is why W6b-3 filed it rather than picking one: `CLAUDE.md` §0 makes a capability no requirement specifies a spec change, not code invented at a call site. The same gap will reach every audit-trail, approval and retention surface `06` specifies, each of which names a principal. Raised 2026-08-25 (W6b-3). Mirrored in `docs/specs/00-overview.md` §10.~~ | **(a) An owner display on `Dataset`** — the list endpoint resolves `owner_id` to a name and returns it alongside, as `latest_version_status` already rides on an existing query. Smallest change, no new route, and it composes with the batching the list already does. But it answers only for `Dataset`, and every other entity naming a principal needs the same field added again. **(b) A principal-lookup route** — `GET /api/v1/users/{id}` or similar. General, and the obvious shape. Costs a round trip per distinct principal on a page, or an N+1 the frontend has to dedupe itself, and exposes a directory surface that `06`'s RBAC has to scope. **(c) A batch principal-name endpoint** — `POST /api/v1/principals/resolve` taking ids and returning display names. One request per page regardless of row count, which is the access pattern a list actually has; the most machinery, and a new artifact shape to specify. **(d) Leave it a uuid.** Honest and stable, and it is what ships today; it makes every ownership, approval and audit screen unreadable by the people those screens are for. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c), with (a) as the near-term step — a principal-resolution route, and FR-82's display amended to the resolved name** |  |  |  |
| OQ-552 | open question |  | ~~How does the SPA render a~~ **principal it did not authenticate as**? W6b-3 added `01` §5.3's owner column and could only render `owner_id` itself — a bare uuid. FR-82 says the column displays `owner_id` and justifies the field on the ground that `06`'s RBAC and approval trails "need a named subject"; a uuid does not name a subject, so the column satisfies the requirement's sentence while defeating the one that motivates it. Nothing can close the gap: there is no `/api/v1/users` and no principal-lookup route in the published contract, and `/api/v1/me` answers for the caller alone. Nor is there a convention — no `me.ts`, `frontend/src/stores/` still empty, and before W6b-3 no view rendered a principal id anywhere, making this the SPA's **first identity rendering**. `CLAUDE.md` §0 therefore makes it a spec change rather than code invented at a call site, which is why W6b-3 filed it instead of picking. The same gap reaches every audit-trail, approval and retention surface `06` specifies. Options are an owner display on `Dataset`, a principal-lookup route, a batch resolve endpoint, or leaving the uuid; the recommendation is the batch endpoint, because every place this appears is a list of rows each naming a principal. **Open**, owned by the maintainer as a decision with `06`'s RBAC scope as an input; options, trade-offs and reasoning are in `docs/open-questions.md`. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c), with (a) as the near-term step — a principal-resolution route, and FR-82's display amended to the resolved name** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-553 | open question |  | ~~**`artifact-envelope`'s two sides disagree on whether `updated_at` is nullable, and no artifact emits the field, so nothing can settle it from behaviour.** The hand-authored `docs/contracts/schemas/common/artifact-envelope.schema.json` types it `{"type": "string", "format": "date-time"}`; the generated side publishes `anyOf: [date-time string, null]` with `default: null`, from `packages/model-schema/src/model_schema/envelope.py:38` (`updated_at: datetime \| None = None`). The `required` lists are identical on both sides, so this is a type disagreement and not a presence one. `00-overview.md` §4.3 sides with the authored form, and does so by its own convention: it writes `"updated_at": "timestamptz"` while `archived_at`, `parent_id` and `description` in the same block each carry `\|null`. **Raised 2026-08-25 (WK-664), on the branch that first made this pair compare at all** — `artifact-envelope` is authored under `common/`, and `test_every_eligible_schema_is_compared` resolved authored paths flat, so the two sides had never been placed side by side (OQ-649's correction note carries the census). **Why this is a question rather than a fix: neither side is the tested one.** `ArtifactEnvelope` has no producer — a definition, a re-export, `scripts/generate-contracts.py:44`, and one construction in `packages/model-schema/tests/test_refs.py:40` — which makes the Pydantic default an undeclared-by-anything default rather than observed behaviour; and the authored schema is `allOf`-composed by twelve artifact schemas, which makes it the form an external reader has actually been given. Against both, `updated_at` exists on exactly two backend tables — `WorkspaceSettingRow` (`backend/src/app/db/models.py:516`) and `ApprovalPolicyRow` (`:682`), both `nullable=False` with `server_default=func.now()` — and **neither is an artifact and neither composes the envelope**, so not one of the twelve composers carries the column at all. The field is specified-and-unbuilt, and the first producer written against either form settles it silently unless it is decided first.~~ | **(a) Non-null on both sides.** Drop `\| None` in `envelope.py`; the authored contract, §4.3 and the twelve composers already read that way. Cost: every producer must then supply a value, and for an artifact that has never been updated the only honest one is `created_at`, so the field carries no information until it does. **(b) Nullable on both sides.** Widen the authored schema and amend §4.3 to `timestamptz\|null`. Cost: it deletes the specification to make the tooling agree — the direction `CLAUDE.md` §0 names explicitly, and which OQ-568's row already refuses for this same reason — on the authority of a default no code has exercised. **(c) Decide it when the first envelope producer is written, and hold the comparison silent on that one path until then.** Cost: a pinned exemption, and the discipline to delete it. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) pinned — the artifact-envelope updated_at disagreement stays unresolved until the first artifact that persists an envelope** |  |  |  |
| OQ-553 | open question |  | ~~`artifact-envelope` types `updated_at` non-null in the hand-authored contract and nullable-with-`default: null` in the generated one, while the `required` lists agree — a type disagreement on a field~~ **no artifact emits**. §4.3 sides with the authored form by its own convention (`archived_at`, `parent_id` and `description` carry `\|null`; `updated_at` does not). Neither side is the tested one: `ArtifactEnvelope` has no producer, the authored schema is `allOf`-composed by twelve artifact schemas, and the only two backend tables carrying an `updated_at` are not artifacts and do not compose the envelope. Held pinned in `UNRESOLVED_TYPE_DISAGREEMENTS` until the first envelope producer settles it. Raised 2026-08-25 (WK-664). **Open** — options, trade-offs and a recommendation (hold the path pinned; delete the pin at the first envelope producer) are in `docs/open-questions.md`. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) pinned — the artifact-envelope updated_at disagreement stays unresolved until the first artifact that persists an envelope** **RESOLVED 2026-08-26 (W6b-19): the condition fired — `DatasetVersion` is the first artifact that persists an envelope — and option (a) was applied: `updated_at` is non-null on both sides; the pin was released, deleted not relaxed.** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-554 | open question |  | **Nothing checks that an `FR-` id a workflow step cites contains what the step claims.** Two mechanisms score workflow citations — `audit-docs.py` check 14 scores whether a journey *mentions* a requirement id, and check 21 whether an endpoint or function a journey cites is declared in the owning spec's §5.1 or §5.2. Neither reads the requirement's text: a step citing `FR-171` for double lift passes both checks on a tree where the requirement struck double lift on 2026-08-17. Raised 2026-08-27 (RFC-778 item (f)). Mirrored in `docs/specs/00-overview.md` §10. | **(a) A structural check** — the audit reads the cited requirement's clauses, including dated amendments, for the instrument the step names, and fails a step that cites it for content the requirement no longer carries. Sharpest, and the hardest: requirements are prose, so the check needs a per-requirement instrument inventory, which becomes a maintained list (the `RFC-756` duplication pattern). **(b) A review obligation at each close** — the §13 close adds a step: for every workflow step the workstream's journey cites, verify the cited requirement still says what the step claims. Cheap, manual, and it lands where the drift is created. **(c) Accept the gap and record it** — this row is the record; nothing changes, and the failure item (f) describes stays. |  |  |  |
| OQ-554 | open question |  | Nothing checks that an `FR-` id cited by a workflow step contains what the step claims (checks 14 and 21 score mentions and declarations, never content). Raised 2026-08-27 (RFC-778 item (f)). Mirrored in `docs/open-questions.md`. Status: **open**. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-555 | open question |  | ~~**`audit-docs.py` check 32's citation-resolution branch will fail the id standard's own specimen ids once `docs/INDEX.md` exists.** RFC-937 §1.1 rule 3, and its verbatim lift at `docs/process/document-ids.md`, teach the padding equivalence by writing one plan id three ways — unpadded, padded to the standard's width, and over-padded — and saying the resolver treats them as one id. No such plan exists, so check 32 (`citation_problems_in_file`, `scripts/audit-docs.py:1554-1556`) reports each as *does not resolve in docs/INDEX.md*. The whole check is gated on that index existing and so is quiet today; it fires the moment the RFC-937 migration lands. RL-1044 §1.2 rules the sibling *padding* branch by requiring a padded id to resolve before it counts as a citation, which cannot be applied here: gating the dangling-citation check on the citation resolving would delete the check. Raised 2026-09-03 by the decision-maker out of RL-1044 §1.7. Mirrored in `docs/specs/00-overview.md` §10. **Deliberately not a findings-register row, so it is not "fixed" by being moved there:** the register's columns are `Finding id`, `Concerns`, `Work item`, `Phase`, `Decision`, with no cell for options, trade-offs or a recommendation, and `CLAUDE.md` §10 requires all three of an open design choice; and its `Decision` vocabulary (`fix before close`, `accept`, `carry forward`, `split verdict`) states dispositions of an established defect. F68, F96, F99 and F100 are defects measured with a right answer. This has none yet, which is the discriminator.~~ **DECIDED 2026-09-03 — option (a), against the recommendation below: the standard's specimens become placeholders.** RFC-937 §1.1 rule 3 and its verbatim lift at `docs/process/document-ids.md` now write `PL-<nnnnn>-<slug>.md` for the filename and `PL-<n>` / `PL-0<n>` / `PL-00<n>` for the three spellings, so no specimen in the standard can ever resolve through `docs/INDEX.md`, and each file carries a dated amendment note recording the change. Ruled by the maintainer under standing delegation as RL-1046 D6 (`docs/rulings/RL-01046-the-alias-class-the-disclosed-rows-and-the-run-s-conditional-window.md`). | **(a) The standard's literal specimens become placeholders** (`PL-<nnnnn>`), as its ADR filename examples already are. Needs no new mechanism, but it cannot express the rule it is applied to: rule 3's sentence exists to say that three *different* spellings are one id, and a single placeholder cannot be written three ways. It also repeats the check-19 remedy, where the maintainer's literal wording was altered to satisfy a check. **(b) A reserved number band `doc-id.py next` never allocates**, so a specimen resolves to nothing permanently. Robust, but it is a standard change for a lint's benefit and the band is a constant with no reader — nothing fails if it is forgotten. **(c) A reviewed per-file exemption for the resolution branch only**, in the ratified shape of `_SPECIFICATIONS_OF_THE_OLD_PATH` (`tests/test_notes_move_citations.py:117-127`): a named list, one reviewed line per document, with an explicit prohibition on widening the pattern. Known cost: the list grows, which F71 records a rate for rather than an opinion about. |  |  |  |
| OQ-555 | open question |  | ~~`audit-docs.py` check 32's citation-resolution branch will fail the id standard's own specimen ids (the three spellings of one plan id in RFC-937 §1.1 rule 3) as unresolvable citations once `docs/INDEX.md` exists; RL-1044 §1.2's resolve-first rule fixes the sibling padding branch and cannot fix this one. Raised 2026-09-03 out of RL-1044 §1.7.~~ **DECIDED 2026-09-03 — option (a): RFC-937 §1.1 rule 3 and its lift at `docs/process/document-ids.md` write their specimens as placeholders (`PL-<nnnnn>-<slug>.md`, `PL-<n>`, `PL-0<n>`, `PL-00<n>`), so no specimen can resolve through `docs/INDEX.md`** (RL-1046 D6). Mirrored in `docs/open-questions.md`. Status: **decided**. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-556 | open question |  | ~~`01` §5.3's PSI comparison selector needs somewhere to keep the chosen reference version. §5.3's own note and `docs/roadmap.md`'s Pinia row both predict the frontend's first **Pinia store**, on the premise that this is "the first piece of Profile state that must outlive a route". Is that premise right, or does the reference belong in the **route query**?~~ | Raised 2026-08-19 (WK-661, the comparison-selector slice), by checking the premise rather than inheriting it. **Nothing requires the reference to survive navigation** — not FR-63, not §5.3, not any other requirement; the prediction was made before the selector had a design. **Route query `?against=<version>`:** vue-router already owns URL state, so no new state layer; the comparison becomes a link an actuary can send to a colleague, and survives reload. Costs the first `useRoute`/`useRouter` in an app where every view so far takes props, and a `vue-router` mock in one file, `ProfileView.test.ts`, under which its eleven existing tests then run. **Pinia store:** survives navigation between profile routes and matches the prediction, but is global state for a single consumer, is **not shareable**, and is lost on reload. **Local ref:** smallest surface, no persistence at all. |  |  |  |
| OQ-556 | open question |  | ~~§5.3's PSI comparison selector needs somewhere to keep the chosen reference version. §5.3's own note and `docs/roadmap.md`'s Pinia row both predict the frontend's first Pinia store, on the premise that this is "the first piece of Profile state that must outlive a route". Is that premise right, or does the reference belong in the route query?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-19: the route query (`?against=<version>`), not a Pinia store.** Nothing requires the reference to survive navigation — the premise behind the store prediction does not hold once checked — and the route query costs the first `useRoute`/`useRouter` in the frontend rather than the first Pinia store; `frontend/src/stores/` stays empty. Raised 2026-08-19 (WK-661, the comparison-selector slice). |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-557 | open question |  | ~~Large-loss handling: preparation step or modelling decision?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: modelling decision, applied at fit time.** | Delegated to Claude. Datasets are immutable versioned artifacts, so baking a cap in means a new Dataset Version — plus re-validation and re-profiling — for every capping assumption. Actuaries routinely try several caps when fitting severity, so that cost lands on the most common inner loop in the project. |  |  |  |
| OQ-557 | open question |  | ~~Should large-loss handling (capping, spreading above a threshold) be a *preparation* step baked into the dataset, or a *modelling* decision applied at fit time? Baking it in makes exposure/claims totals consistent everywhere; applying it at fit time lets one dataset serve multiple capping assumptions.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: a modelling decision, applied at fit time.** A Model Spec carries `loss_treatment` in `spec_hash` so two models differing only in their cap cannot collide, and the Peril Structure reconciles after restoration against uncapped observed data — one validated dataset serves many capping assumptions (FR-127/128). |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-558 | open question |  | ~~Incremental/append ingestion, or full snapshot per version?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: full snapshots for Phase 1; append deferred to Phase 2 under a stated constraint.** | Delegated to Claude. Phase 1a's demo dataset is freMTPL2 — small enough that snapshots are free. Append would add real complexity now (validation must reason about which parts are new; profiling and PSI would need incremental variants) to solve a problem the project does not yet have. |  |  |  |
| OQ-558 | open question |  | ~~Do we support incremental/append ingestion (adding a new month to an existing version) or is every version a full snapshot? Full snapshots are simpler and match immutability; append is far cheaper for a 10-year book refreshed monthly.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: full snapshots for Phase 1; append deferred to Phase 2 under a stated constraint.** Content-addressing already deduplicates unchanged parts, so most of append's saving exists without an append mode at all (FR-27, FR-34). |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-559 | open question |  | ~~Keep the `sql` custom validation check?
