| OQ-631 | open question |  | ~~What is the right default cadence set? Weekly for A/E is too frequent to be meaningful on low-frequency perils but monthly is too slow to catch a broken feed. Likely different cadences per metric family — needs to be specified rather than left to the user.~~ **Open** — the recommendation is per-family cadences (`input_drift` daily, `model_ae` monthly with a maturity floor) as the Monitor's template defaults; options and trade-offs are in `docs/open-questions.md`. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: per family: input_drift daily, operational hourly, price_portfolio weekly, demand weekly, model_ae monthly with a 6-month maturity floor** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-632 | open question |  | ~~Does an `expression` Custom Objective need its own authoring permission, distinct from `model:fit`?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes — `custom_objective:author`, granted by no built-in role by default.** | Raised 2026-08-18 (WK-661) by superseding `custom_objective:author`/`custom_objective:submit` in `06` §4.1: Phase 1's objectives are the §4.5 template catalogue, so authoring one is choosing how a model is fitted and `model:fit` is the right check. A Phase 2 `expression` objective is different in kind — author-written maths that a reviewer must read, whose blast radius (FR-164) spans every model using it. **Separate permission**: an actuary may fit without being trusted to write a loss; costs a role migration and a screen that explains why a control is hidden. **Keep `model:fit`**: no new vocabulary, and the real gate stays where it already is — certification (FR-146) plus a non-author Approver (FR-163). |  |  |  |
| OQ-632 | open question |  | ~~Does an `expression` Custom Objective (Phase 2, `02` FR-150) need an authoring permission distinct from `model:fit`?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes — `custom_objective:author`, granted by no built-in role's default set — FR-367**, discharging FR-366's trigger before WK-690 rather than at it. Template selection stays `model:fit`; submission stays `model:submit`. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-633 | open question |  | ~~Audit hash chain: per workspace or global, and is a self-held chain enough?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: per workspace and self-held, claimed as tamper-evident and never tamper-proof, with an optional chain-head anchor.** | A chain the platform operator controls end to end detects *storage-layer* tampering but not a determined operator who can recompute it. External anchoring (publishing the chain head to an append-only external store, a customer-controlled bucket, or a transparency log) closes that gap at the cost of an external dependency and an operational ritual. |  |  |  |
| OQ-633 | open question |  | ~~Audit hash chain: per workspace or global, and is a self-held chain enough?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: per workspace, self-held, described as tamper-evident rather than tamper-proof, with an explicit chain-head anchor operation the customer automates into a store the operator does not control — FR-373.** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-634 | open question |  | ~~Are platform roles authoritative, or is IdP group membership?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: hybrid — the IdP owns identity and role membership, the platform owns scope.** | IdP-authoritative: joiner/leaver processes work automatically, which large insurers require; but artifact-scoped permissions (FR-345) do not map cleanly to IdP groups. Platform-authoritative: precise scoping, and a leaver keeps access until someone remembers. |  |  |  |
| OQ-634 | open question |  | ~~Are platform roles authoritative, or is IdP group membership?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: hybrid — the IdP owns identity and role membership so a leaver loses access automatically; the platform owns artifact scope, which names objects no directory has heard of — FR-350.** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-635 | open question |  | ~~Can an Admin override a flag (FR-359)?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes, made maximally expensive and permanently visible.** | Allowing it creates a single bypass of every gate the platform has, which is exactly what an auditor will look for. Disallowing it means a false-positive flag — a genuinely invalidated dataset that was re-validated, a transparency artifact that failed to generate — blocks a legitimate change with no recourse. |  |  |  |
| OQ-635 | open question |  | ~~Can an Admin override a flag (FR-359)?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes, and it leaves a scar — two Admins, a written justification, a badge on the artifact that never expires, the exception log, and its own line in the approval history — FR-360.** An override never clears the underlying flag. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-636 | open question |  | ~~Do we need formal model risk tiering?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes — `risk_tier` on Model Family and Rating Algorithm, which the Approval Policy may key on.** | Tiering (tier 1 models needing more approvers and more frequent attestation) is standard in UK insurer model governance and maps poorly onto per-artifact-type policy, which cannot distinguish a headline motor frequency model from a windscreen burning-cost model. |  |  |  |
| OQ-636 | open question |  | ~~Do we need formal model risk tiering?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: yes — `risk_tier` on Model Family and Rating Algorithm, which `ApprovalPolicy` entries may key on — FR-365.** On the family and the algorithm rather than the version, so it cannot be lowered on the day a quicker approval is wanted. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-637 | open question |  | ~~How much commentary is required before an approval can be submitted?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: exactly two Commentary Blocks, with a minimum length and no default text.** | Requiring commentary in defined dossier slots (purpose, limitations) improves documentation and reliably produces boilerplate. Requiring none produces dossiers that are all numbers and no reasoning — precisely what a reviewer complains about. |  |  |  |
| OQ-637 | open question |  | ~~How much commentary is required before an approval can be submitted?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: exactly two Commentary Blocks — §4.4 §1 and §15 — with a minimum length and no default text to accept — FR-378.** Ten mandatory slots produce ten paragraphs of boilerplate; two produce two paragraphs somebody wrote. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-638 | open question |  | ~~Does TAS 200 (Insurance) cover pricing and premium rating, or only reserving, capital and Solvency II actuarial-function work?~~ **DETERMINED 2026-08-18 by reading TAS 200 v2.0: it covers pricing, via the `Pricing frameworks` scope item.** | TAS 100 applies regardless — it is the general standard. TAS 200 is the insurance-specific layer on top, revised to v2.0 (published 2024-09-20, effective 2025-01-01) with strengthened **assumption-setting** requirements and new material on the FCA's Consumer Duty. If pricing is in scope, `02`'s factor/banding/grouping rationale and `01`'s validation evidence must meet those requirements and `06` §4.4's dossier sections may need to expand; if not, TAS 100 alone governs and the bar is lower. The scope statement is in the standard text and could not be established from the FRC's public summaries. |  |  |  |
| OQ-638 | open question |  | ~~Does TAS 200 (Insurance) cover pricing and premium rating, or only reserving, capital and Solvency II actuarial-function work?~~ **DETERMINED 2026-08-18 by reading TAS 200 v2.0: it applies, through the `Pricing frameworks` scope item, whose glossary definition names the methodologies, assumptions and models behind an insurer's premium rates — FR-362.** No pricing-specific provisions section exists, so §1's P1.1–P1.4 bind, plus TAS 100. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-639 | open question |  | ~~Does `06` §3.3's per-artifact evidence table or `06` §4.2's `ApprovalPolicy` defaults decide what a submission actually requires?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: §3.3 is a floor per artifact type; §4.2 may add to it and never remove from it.** | Raised 2026-08-17 (WK-661, the model lifecycle) by making `EVIDENCE_INCOMPLETE` a refusal something raises: the check has to read a list, and there are two. **§3.3 is the requirement, §4.2 is the configuration** — plausible, and it makes every workspace's editable policy unable to *relax* a floor it cannot see; a submission would then be refused for missing evidence the policy does not mention, which is the error message nobody can act on. **§4.2 is the whole answer** — what the code does now, and it means an insurer can edit its way to approving a GBM with no transparency artifact, which `02` R3 forbids outright. **§3.3 is a floor, §4.2 may only add to it** — the two documents then say different things without contradicting each other, at the cost of a rule that lives in neither table and must be written down a third time. |  |  |  |
| OQ-639 | open question |  | ~~Does `06` §3.3's per-artifact evidence table or `06` §4.2's `ApprovalPolicy` defaults decide what a submission actually requires?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: §3.3 is a floor per artifact type and §4.2 may only add to it — FR-364**, with the floor restated in §4.2 so a reader of the defaults sees it, refused at policy save, and applied as a union at submission. The enforced floor is §3.3's checkable projection; the uncheckable remainder is named with an owner. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-640 | open question |  | ~~Celery vs a transactional Postgres queue.~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: Celery.** | Maintainer chose Celery over the Postgres-queue prototype this file recommended. Reasonable: Celery is mature, Redis is already in the stack, and the throughput headroom is real. |  |  |  |
| OQ-640 | open question |  | ~~Celery or a Postgres-backed queue?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-14: Celery.** The maintainer chose Celery over the Postgres-queue prototype this spec had recommended. That is a reasonable call — Celery is mature, Redis is already in the stack, and the throughput headroom is real. It does, however, mean the transactional-enqueue property a Postgres queue would have given for free must now be built: `FR-406` requires a transactional outbox, because `06` R2 makes an un-audited job a correctness problem rather than an inconvenience. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-641 | open question |  | ~~Does Dagster earn its place?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: no — it is dropped, and scheduled work is the tick specified as `07` FR-413.** | It brings partitioned assets and backfills that `05` genuinely needs (NFR-517) and that would otherwise be hand-rolled badly. It also brings a second scheduler, a second UI, and a second operational surface next to the Jobs model. |  |  |  |
| OQ-641 | open question |  | ~~Does Dagster earn its place, or should scheduled work be Celery beat plus a thin scheduler?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: it does not — Dagster is dropped, and scheduled work is the tick specified as `FR-413`.** The recommendation on file was "keep it, but confine it to scheduling and partitioning", with a fallback to beat *if the boundary proves hard to hold*. Confining it to scheduling is what settles it: once Dagster may not execute pricing work, what remains of it is a clock and a table of which periods have run — and the platform must own both anyway, because a scheduled run has to be a Job (FR-411) with the same audit, cancellation and outbox guarantees (FR-406) as any other. Keeping Dagster buys a UI for state the Jobs UI must already show, and costs a second scheduler, a second operational surface and a second place a run can exist. **The two capabilities cited for it are specified directly rather than lost**: catch-up, because schedules are period-anchored rather than interval-timed, and backfill for a Monitor created late (`05` NFR-517), as an explicit bounded request. Nothing is un-built by this — Dagster was never a dependency (absent from `pyproject.toml` and `uv.lock`), so the decision removes a plan, not code. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-642 | open question |  | ~~Is the scoring service a separate application image?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: one image with a role flag through Phases 1–2, a separate scoring image from Phase 3 — and the boundary that keeps the split cheap is enforced from today.** | Separate: much smaller attack surface and dependency set on the latency path, faster cold start, and it makes NFR-534 structural rather than aspirational. Same image with a role flag: one build, one version, simpler release. |  |  |  |
| OQ-642 | open question |  | ~~Should the scoring service be a separate deployable *application*, rather than the same image run with a different role?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-18: one image with a role flag through Phases 1–2, a separate scoring image from Phase 3** — and the thing that makes the later split cheap is specified now as `NFR-535`, which scores a Model in a process where the fitting stack cannot be imported, and so stops that path growing a dependency the split would later have to shed. *An import-linter contract was tried first and is the wrong instrument — the requirement records why.* The image itself is Phase 3 (WK-674's successor); the boundary is enforced from today. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-643 | open question |  | ~~Are workspace resource quotas needed at all?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: no — none is specified or built (`07` FR-415).** | In a single-tenant deployment (FR-16) they mostly protect users from themselves — a 40-factor GBM on 50 M rows that starves the scoring workers. Not needed for isolation. |  |  |  |
| OQ-643 | open question |  | ~~How are workspace-level resource quotas expressed (concurrent compute jobs, total storage)?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: they are not — no Workspace quota is specified or built, and `FR-415` records the boundary that replaces it.** The recommendation on file was a *deferral* — skip quotas "until multi-tenancy is on the table (OQ-540)" — and that trigger cannot fire, because OQ-540 was itself decided on 2026-08-15 (ADR-710, FR-16) as one tenant per deployment, permanently. A deferral whose trigger is already dead is a rejection nobody has written down, so it is written down here instead. What the ADR left genuinely open is answered the same way: a tenant's own scoring path is protected from its own modelling workload by the queue split (FR-405, FR-434), not by a per-workspace number. The half that stays open is *enforcement*, not expression — FR-412's memory budget and a worker service for it to apply to are **WK-674**'s. Original text: **Half of this question was already answered:** ADR-710 makes every deployment single-tenant, so a quota never protects one insurer from another — it protects a tenant's own scoring path from its own modelling workload. What remains open is whether that needs expressing per workspace at all, or whether the worker pool split (FR-434) already does it. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-644 | open question |  | ~~How does the **browser** authenticate?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-15: authorization code + PKCE in the SPA, for Phases 1–2.** | The SPA sent no credential at all, and the platform refuses an unauthenticated request (`07` §3.7) — so a browser got 401 on everything while every view and its tests passed, because the tests stub `fetch` and nothing exercised the real transport. The two real options were **PKCE in the SPA** (no new server component, standard for a public client; a token in JS is XSS-reachable and silent renewal is fiddly) and a **BFF session cookie** (token stays server-side, `SameSite`/`HttpOnly` removes the XSS reach, at the cost of a session-holding component the platform does not otherwise need). |  |  |  |
| OQ-644 | open question |  | ~~How does the browser authenticate to the API — authorization-code + PKCE in the SPA, or a BFF session cookie?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-15: PKCE in the SPA for Phases 1–2**, specified as FR-393 and owned by WK-664. Revisit at Phase 3 if a deployment requires that no token exist in the browser at all. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-645 | open question |  | ~~Bundle an OIDC provider in the production stack?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: local development yes, behind an opt-in profile; production no.** | Bundling Keycloak lowers the barrier for a small insurer with no IdP, and makes us responsible for operating and patching an identity provider — the highest-consequence component to get wrong. |  |  |  |
| OQ-645 | open question |  | ~~Do we ship a bundled OIDC provider (Keycloak) in the production compose stack as a supported deployment, or is an external IdP always required outside local development?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: bundled for local development behind an opt-in compose profile (`FR-398`), never in production (`FR-437`).** The two halves are separate questions that the one word "bundle" hid. Locally there is a real hole — `FR-387` said a provider runs in the local stack in the present indicative and none does, so the only way in is `dev_auth_enabled`, and the browser flow WK-664 must build (FR-393) has nothing to authenticate against. In production the answer is the recommendation's: `deploy/` carries a *reference* Keycloak deployment the deployer operates and patches, and operating an identity provider on an insurer's behalf is not a responsibility this project takes. The local provider is an **alternative** to `dev_auth_enabled`, never a replacement — both test suites still run with no container. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-646 | open question |  | ~~A `PlatformError` raised inside a Job handler loses its `.code` before it is stored. Should `execute_job`'s generic exception handler preserve it, and how?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-22: (a) — a dedicated `except PlatformError` clause before the generic one, storing the handler's own code, with retry behaviour unchanged.** | `backend/src/app/worker/tasks.py`'s `except Exception` around the handler call stores `JobError(code="JOB_HANDLER_FAILED", message=f"{type(exc).__name__}: {exc}")` for *every* unexpected exception, `PlatformError` included. `PlatformError.__init__` (`backend/src/app/errors.py`) calls `super().__init__(detail or title)`, so `str(exc)` is the error's title or detail — never its `.code` — and the stored message is not even a substring match for the code that was raised. Found on the FR-137 branch: Task 4's two refusal tests (`test_the_transparency_job_refuses_a_hand_written_spec_that_disagrees_with_its_source`-shaped tests) had to call the handler function directly rather than go through `execute_job`, because no test going through the real dispatch path can assert on a specific error code from any handler — every handler's `PlatformError` looks identical to every other's once it reaches `JobError`. **(a)** add a dedicated `except PlatformError as exc` clause before the generic one, storing `JobError(code=exc.code, message=exc.detail or exc.title, ...)` — the handler's own code survives, and only a genuinely unexpected exception falls through to `JOB_HANDLER_FAILED`. **(b)** leave `JOB_HANDLER_FAILED` as the stored code for every handler failure, but put the original code in `JobError.detail` as a structured field — smaller diff, but a caller reading only `.code` (which is the contract's documented field) still cannot branch on it. **(c)** leave it as is, and require every negative test to call the handler directly — costs nothing today, but it is a standing gap for every future handler-raised `PlatformError`, not only this branch's two. |  |  |  |
| OQ-646 | open question |  | ~~A `PlatformError` raised inside a Job handler loses its `.code` before it reaches storage. Should the generic handler special-case `PlatformError` to preserve it?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-22: yes — option (a), a dedicated `except PlatformError` clause before the generic one, storing `JobError(code=exc.code, message=exc.detail or exc.title, retryable=False, trace_id=…)`.** `JOB_HANDLER_FAILED` stays exactly where it belongs, as the code for a genuinely unexpected exception, and no retry behaviour changed — `retryable` is the same literal `False`, and the clause deliberately does not infer retryability from `status_code`. **The ordering that matters is not the one the question implied.** `PlatformError`, `JobCancelled` and `JobBudgetExceededError` are three independent direct subclasses of `Exception`, checked against the MRO rather than assumed, so the clauses already there are genuine siblings and their order is free; `except Exception` is a *base* of `PlatformError`, and a clause placed after it would never run while looking correct. FR-403 is what this restores — the typed error code a failed Job records — and three tests in `backend/tests/test_model_jobs.py` carry that mark: a named-code test through `execute_job`, a `RuntimeError` control proving `JOB_HANDLER_FAILED` is still reached, and a 429 `RATE_LIMITED` case proving `retryable` is still `False`. **Not delivered, and not disguised:** `PlatformError.errors` still does not reach `JobError.detail`, so FR-403's field-level-cause clause remains unmet for handler-raised errors. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-647 | open question |  | *(raised 2026-08-23)* ~~What does an `Idempotency-Key` naming an already-finished Job mean, and does FR-404's 24-hour window exist?~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: option (c) — the window is withdrawn, keys are permanent, and a terminally failed Job releases its key. Specified as `07` FR-414.** | FR-404 specifies a 24-hour window; `platform.jobs.submit()` applies none and returns the existing Job whatever its status. A failed Job's key is therefore spent forever and a succeeded one is honoured indefinitely — the sentence is unimplemented in both directions at once. FR-413 makes it load-bearing: `(schedule_id, period)` is exactly a key whose Job may have failed. |  |  |  |
| OQ-647 | open question |  | *(raised 2026-08-23 by OQ-641)* ~~**What does an `Idempotency-Key` that names a Job which already finished mean, and does the 24-hour window in FR-404 exist?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: option (c) — the window is withdrawn, keys are permanent, and a terminally failed Job releases its key. Specified as FR-414.** FR-404 says a key matching a Job "from the last 24 h" returns the original. `platform.jobs.submit()` applies **no** time window and returns the existing Job whatever its status — so a key whose Job *failed* is spent forever, and a key whose Job succeeded is honoured a year later. Both halves of the spec sentence are therefore unimplemented in opposite directions, and FR-413 depends on the answer: `(schedule_id, period)` is exactly a key whose Job may have failed. Options: **(a)** implement the window as written — simple, but a period older than a day silently re-runs, which is the duplicate the key exists to prevent; **(b)** drop the window, keys permanent — safest against duplicates, leaves no way to retry a failed period except a new key; **(c)** keys permanent, but a **terminally failed** Job releases its key, so a retry under the same key is a fresh Job and a success or a cancellation is not. **(c) was the recommendation and is the decision** — it is the only one where "has this period been done?" and "may it be attempted again?" are different questions, which is what the failed-period case actually needs. The window's withdrawal reaches `00` §5.4, which stated it as a platform-wide convention, and `01` FR-33, whose Ingestion Run key is a second index over the same header; both are amended with the decision. Owner: whichever workstream builds FR-413. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-648 | open question |  | ~~**How does a principal's chosen workspace reach the API?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: option (a) — a verified `Workspace-Id` header, specified as `07` FR-397.** FR-396 (2026-08-23) requires a multi-membership principal to choose, and the choice to be verified against its own memberships on every request; it does not say how the choice is transported. Raised by the WK-664 slice-map backlog, item 2. | The question is live rather than a formality because the codebase already argues one way: the request-scope dependency carries the comment that a header-supplied workspace "would make the scope a claim rather than a fact", repeated on the job path — and today a principal with several memberships is refused outright. **(a) A verified header.** The browser sends the selection; the dependency checks it against the memberships the identity already holds and refuses a value that is not among them. Cheap, one code path, and the invariant's objection arguably does not reach it — a choice among facts the platform holds is not a claim. But every caller must remember to send it, and a forgotten header is a request that silently runs somewhere. **(b) A path prefix** — `/workspaces/{id}/…` on every scoped route. The workspace becomes part of the resource identity, so a URL is complete: shareable, loggable, and impossible to forget. It is the largest change, touching every route and the generated client. **(c) Bound into the session at login.** The selection is made once, stored server-side, and no request carries it. Nothing to forge and nothing to forget, but a principal cannot hold two workspaces open in two tabs, and switching becomes a stateful operation rather than a parameter. |  |  |  |
| OQ-648 | open question |  | ~~**How does a principal's chosen workspace reach the API?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-23: option (a) — a verified `Workspace-Id` header, specified as FR-397.** FR-396 requires the choice to be verified against membership on every request and deliberately does not say how it is carried. Raised 2026-08-23 by the WK-664 slice-map backlog (item 2). The obvious answer — a request header — is argued against by an invariant in the code itself, that a header-supplied workspace "would make the scope a claim rather than a fact". Options and the reasoning (a verified header, a `/workspaces/{id}/…` path prefix, or the workspace bound into the session at login) are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). The invariant that made the question live is satisfied rather than overridden: the header is checked against membership, and an absent one is refused rather than defaulted. `W6b-11` is unblocked as a decision and still waits on WK-692's backend half; nothing was unsafe meanwhile, because the API refuses a multi-membership principal. *Amended 2026-08-25: WK-692's backend half landed (`backend/src/app/api/deps.py`, migration `57547846f0a3`, covered by `backend/tests/test_workspace_selection.py`), so `W6b-11` waits only on its own dependency `W6b-10`. The last clause described the code on 2026-08-23: the API no longer refuses every multi-membership principal, it refuses one that sends no selection and one naming a workspace it is not a member of. Mirrored in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md).* |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-649 | open question |  | ~~**Every layer of the contract guard is scoped to the intersection of its two sides, so a shape or keyword present on only one side is outside all of them by construction.** The type comparison intersects paths; the constraint comparison intersects paths and then keywords; `test_every_eligible_schema_is_compared` defines `eligible` as generated ∩ authored, so the completeness check is itself defined over the complement of the problem. The consequence that makes this a design question rather than a bug: **the guard is silent in exactly the same way whether a shape is one-sided deliberately or accidentally.** `model-comparison`, `backtest`, `objective-usage`, `custom-metric` and `metric-certificate` are generated-only on purpose — a shape's first written form, and `generate-contracts.py` says so in a comment beside each. **That those comments are themselves unchecked is part of the finding**: the one beside `peril-structure` still reads "No hand-authored Phase-0 counterpart" although an authored side was added in #133, so the only existing declaration of intent has already gone stale without anything noticing — which is the argument for deriving the declaration rather than narrating it in a comment. A shape whose authored side was deleted or never written would present identically, and no reader or test can tell the two apart. **And the blind spot moves.** The intersection is recomputed from whatever the two sides hold at the moment of the run, and the guard keeps no memory of what it compared last time — so a path that *leaves* the shared set is indistinguishable from one that was never in it. The comparison simply covers one path fewer and stays green. That is strictly worse than static one-sidedness: coverage can shrink one path at a time, indefinitely, with every check passing, and the shrinking is itself the thing nothing reports. A worked instance at discriminator-arm granularity, measured at `7e09eb4` once W32-1b landed and reproducible against that tree: in `model-spec`, moving `family` from the glm arm to the xgboost arm of the *authored* contract takes the type comparison from 146 compared `(arm, path)` keys to 145 with disagreements at **0 before and 0 after**. `family` is shared in the `model_type=glm` arm before the move and shared in **no arm at all** after it — the path does not disagree, it stops being compared, and every check stays green. This is the one case arm attribution does *not* close: W32-1b gives arm-level type and bound disagreement on paths shared *within* an arm, and a field that leaves the shared set entirely is this question rather than that slice's. Note that the shrink is one key out of 146, which is why a threshold expressed as a fraction of the walker's own output cannot catch it. Note the boundary: W32-1b delivers arm-level **type disagreement on shared paths**; arm-level **existence** is this question and is not covered by it. Measured at `946725f`: 26 authored, 25 generated, 15 on both sides, 11 authored-only, 10 generated-only. `validation-report` demonstrates it on a slug added the same day — `empty_layers.[]` is model-only, `overall`, `counts.*` and `results.[].evidence_blob` are contract-only, and the whole suite is green. **Correction 2026-08-25 (WK-664).** The census above is measured over the top level of `docs/contracts/schemas/` only — this question's own blind spot, one level up: `artifact-envelope`, `artifact-ref` and `blob-ref` are authored in `common/` and were counted as generated-only. The corpus at `8a56147` is **31 authored, 25 generated, 18 on both sides, 13 authored-only, 7 generated-only**; the published `26 / 25 / 15 / 11 / 10` is the flat reading. Two consequences. The Recommendation's residual — *all 10 generated-only ones are first-written-forms* — is false as written: three of those ten have authored sides, so the intersection was **silently narrowed rather than deliberately one-sided**, which is the accidental case this question says nothing can distinguish, having occurred here undetected. And once compared, `artifact-envelope`'s two sides disagree on `updated_at` (OQ-553), so the *nothing is wrong today* premise did not hold when it was written. Separately, of the five slugs named above as declaring their one-sidedness in a `generate-contracts.py` comment, **four do**: `metric-certificate`'s comment gives its publication rationale — the ADR-704 boundary — and never says it lacks an authored side, a fact recorded in OQ-651 instead. The comment corpus is therefore both stale (`peril-structure`, already noted) and incomplete, which strengthens (b) rather than changing it.~~ | **(a) Leave it and document the scope.** Honest and free; every count published must then say which of the two frames it means, because "the guard compares everything in scope" and "21 of 36 shapes are out of scope" are both true. Does nothing about the indistinguishability. **(b) Declare one-sidedness rather than infer it.** A slug that is generated-only on purpose says so in one registry; anything one-sided and undeclared fails. Small, and it separates deliberate from accidental — which is the whole finding. Cost: a registry to keep current, and the same staleness risk any curated list carries, mitigated by the entry being derivable from the corpus. **(c) Require both sides for every shape.** Strongest, and wrong here: eleven authored-only slugs are later-phase (governance, optimisation, monitoring, rating), so this builds ahead of the phase for the sake of a guard. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — one-sidedness declared in a registry; anything one-sided and undeclared fails** |  |  |  |
| OQ-649 | open question |  | ~~**Every layer of the contract guard is scoped to the intersection of its two sides, so a shape or keyword present on only one side is outside all of them by construction.**~~ Raised 2026-08-24 (W32-11). The type comparison intersects paths, the constraint comparison intersects paths and then keywords, and the completeness check defines an eligible schema as one with both sides — so it is defined over the complement of the problem. The consequence is not that anything is wrong today but that nothing can tell it apart if it goes wrong: the guard is silent in the same way whether a shape is one-sided **on purpose** — a first written form, which five generated-only slugs are, each said so in a comment — comments nothing checks, and `peril-structure`'s has already gone stale, still denying an authored side that was added in #133 — or **by accident**, an authored side deleted or never written. Measured at `946725f`: 26 authored, 25 generated, 15 both-sided, 11 authored-only, 10 generated-only. Any published count must say which frame it means, because "100% of what is in scope is compared" and "21 of 36 shapes are out of scope" are both true of the same tree. The residual is bounded — all 11 authored-only slugs are later-phase and all 10 generated-only ones are first written forms — which is what makes this a question about keeping that knowable rather than a defect. **The blind spot also moves.** The intersection is recomputed each run and the guard keeps no memory of the previous one, so a path that leaves the shared set is indistinguishable from one never in it — coverage can shrink a path at a time, indefinitely, with every check green. An arm-granularity instance is measured on the unmerged W32-1b branch and will be recorded with its tree once that lands; W32-1b delivers arm-level **type disagreement on shared paths**, not arm-level **existence**, which is this question. The recommendation is to declare one-sidedness rather than infer it; options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). Owner: maintainer. **Correction 2026-08-25 (WK-664).** The census above is measured over the top level of `docs/contracts/schemas/` only — this question's own blind spot, one level up: `artifact-envelope`, `artifact-ref` and `blob-ref` are authored in `common/` and were counted as generated-only. The corpus at `8a56147` is **31 authored, 25 generated, 18 on both sides, 13 authored-only, 7 generated-only**; the published `26 / 25 / 15 / 11 / 10` is the flat reading. Two consequences. The Recommendation's residual — *all 10 generated-only ones are first-written-forms* — is false as written: three of those ten have authored sides, so the intersection was **silently narrowed rather than deliberately one-sided**, which is the accidental case this question says nothing can distinguish, having occurred here undetected. And once compared, `artifact-envelope`'s two sides disagree on `updated_at` (OQ-553), so the *nothing is wrong today* premise did not hold when it was written. Separately, of the five slugs named above as declaring their one-sidedness in a `generate-contracts.py` comment, **four do**: `metric-certificate`'s comment gives its publication rationale — the ADR-704 boundary — and never says it lacks an authored side, a fact recorded in OQ-651 instead. The comment corpus is therefore both stale (`peril-structure`, already noted) and incomplete, which strengthens (b) rather than changing it. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — one-sidedness declared in a registry; anything one-sided and undeclared fails** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-650 | open question |  | ~~**When a shape is tightened, what revalidates the artifacts already stored under the looser one?** Nothing does today, and the read path is where it surfaces. A stored payload is validated on write against whatever the model said that day, then parsed again on read against whatever it says now — so a tightening that is correct going forward can make an existing row unreadable, and the failure appears at read time to a user who did nothing, far from the commit that caused it. The class is not hypothetical in this repository: `OQ-547` moved decimals from accepting JSON numbers to refusing them, and the certificate battery moved from a count floor to an exact set of names in this slice. Both are strictly narrowing, which is the direction that breaks reads.~~ | **(a) Nothing — accept read-time failure.** Free, and defensible only while the corpus is small and pre-production. It fails silently in the direction that matters: the first symptom is an artifact an approver cannot open. **(b) Version the shape and keep readers for old versions.** Correct in general and what a governed artifact store eventually needs, but it is a data-model change across every stored artifact and it buys migration cost on every tightening. **(c) A revalidation sweep, run on demand, that parses every stored artifact against today's models and reports what no longer reads.** Does not prevent the break; makes it visible on the committer's clock instead of the user's, and turns "is anything unreadable?" into a question with an answer. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) now, (b) when artifacts outlive a release** |  |  |  |
| OQ-650 | open question |  | ~~**When a shape is tightened, nothing revalidates the artifacts already stored under the looser one.**~~ Raised 2026-08-24 (W32-11). An artifact is validated on write against the model of that day and parsed again on read against the model of today, so a narrowing that is correct going forward can make an existing row unreadable — and the failure appears at read time, to a user who did nothing, far from the commit that caused it. Two real instances: `OQ-547` moved decimals from accepting JSON numbers to refusing them, and the certificate battery moved from a count floor to an exact set of names (FR-158). Both narrow, which is the direction that breaks reads. The recommendation is an on-demand revalidation sweep now — it does not prevent the break but moves its discovery onto the committer's clock — with per-version readers when artifacts outlive a release; options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). The rule worth writing down either way is that a narrowing change to a stored shape is a migration event, not a model edit. Owner: maintainer. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) now, (b) when artifacts outlive a release** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-651 | open question |  | ~~**`metric-certificate` has a model-side four-check floor and no authored contract at all**, so nothing compares it and nothing publishes it to an external reader. Raised 2026-08-24 as finding F3 of W32-11. It is one of ten generated-only slugs, but unlike the other nine it is not a first written form by design — `objective-certificate` beside it has both sides, and the two are siblings that FR-157 and FR-162 treat alike. **WK-692 goal borne on:** FR-451. **Dispositioned, not delivered:** the floor itself is enforced model-side, which is what FR-157 asks for; only the comparison and the publication are outstanding.~~ | **(a) Author the contract now.** Small and obvious, and it makes the pair symmetric with `objective-certificate`. But writing a new authored contract is authoring a published promise, not comparing an existing pair, and the slice that found it was scoped to two other shapes. **(b) Leave it generated-only and let `OQ-649`'s declaration mechanism cover it.** Cheapest, and wrong in one specific way: a declaration would record it as *deliberately* one-sided, which is the opposite of true — it is one-sided because nobody wrote the other half. **(c) Author it as part of whichever workstream next touches certificates**, so the contract is written by someone with the certificate in front of them. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) — authored by the next workstream that touches certificates** |  |  |  |
| OQ-651 | open question |  | ~~**`metric-certificate` has a model-side four-check floor and no authored contract at all**~~, so nothing compares it and no external reader is told what a metric certificate contains. Raised 2026-08-24 as finding F3 of W32-11. Unlike the nine other generated-only slugs it is not a first written form by design: its sibling `objective-certificate` has both sides, and FR-157 and FR-162 treat the two alike. **WK-692 goal borne on:** FR-451. **Dispositioned, not delivered** — the floor is enforced model-side as FR-157 asks; the comparison and the publication are what is outstanding. The recommendation is to author it in whichever workstream next touches certificates, and the warning worth carrying is that covering it by declaring it deliberately one-sided would record an oversight as a decision. Options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). Owner: maintainer. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) — authored by the next workstream that touches certificates** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-652 | open question |  | ~~**What tells the API that a workspace selection *changed*, so that a switch is audited once rather than never or on every request?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker, the maintainer's delegation): Reading 1 — a switch is a human act; mechanism (c), an explicit `POST /api/v1/me/workspace`.** *(raised 2026-08-24 by W32-7 Task 4.)* FR-396's fourth obligation says a switch is audited into both chains, and W32-7 built the mechanism — `platform.workspace_switch.record_switch` writes one event in the workspace left and one in the workspace entered, in id order so two principals switching in opposite directions cannot deadlock on the per-workspace advisory lock. What W32-7 did **not** build is the call site, and the reason is not effort: `require_caller` runs once per request and holds no memory of the previous one, so "the selection changed" is not a fact it can observe. A switch is a difference between two requests, and the platform stores nothing that spans two requests. | *(options amended 2026-08-24, the day they were filed: `(b)` is split, because its two mechanisms have opposite costs and one of them is worse than the option it would be built instead of.)* **Which option is right turns on a reading of FR-396's fourth obligation that has not been settled, and every cost below flips with it. Reading 1: a switch is a human act. Reading 2: a switch is any difference in effective scope between two consecutive requests by the same principal.** **(a) Audit every selection, with `left=None`.** Needs no new state and loses nothing. It appends one FR-372 event and takes one per-workspace advisory lock (`backend/src/app/platform/audit.py:81-85`) on *every authenticated request a multi-membership principal makes*, turning the chain into a request log — burying the record of what was actually done, which is what the chain is for. **The objection is the chain and the lock, not that a write appears:** the bearer path is already not read-only, since `require_caller` calls `authenticate_bearer` on every request (`backend/src/app/api/deps.py:115`) and `_upsert_user` stamps `last_login_at` each time (`backend/src/app/auth/service.py:146`) — but that is one unlocked column on the user row, appending to no chain and serialising nothing against anyone. Faithful under Reading 2, enormously noisy under Reading 1. **(b1) Store the previous selection on the user row** (`last_workspace_id`). Cheapest to build — the same shape and flush as `last_login_at`, no new table, no expiry. **Measurably worse than (a) in the two-tab case:** a single principal-level "current workspace" cannot represent two concurrently open workspaces, so every request from either tab differs from the stored value and is recorded as a switch, and `record_switch` appends both the workspace entered and the workspace left (`backend/src/app/platform/workspace_switch.py:39-42`) — **two events and two advisory locks per request**, on exactly the two workspaces the tabs are using, so the tabs serialise against each other permanently, where (a) takes one lock on the workspace entered and never contends between them. OQ-648 refused session-bound selection *"on the two-tab case, which is an ordinary thing for an actuary comparing two workspaces to do"*, and FR-397 made the selection per-request so two contexts can hold two workspaces; (b1) reinstates a single current workspace at the principal level. It does not break scoping — the header still governs each request — it corrupts the *record*, silently, in the case the platform went out of its way to support. **(b2) Store it on a session or presence row.** Escapes (b1)'s pathology **only if the stored scope is per-tab**, and nothing the server holds is: a session cookie is shared across tabs, so (b2) degrades to (b1) unless the client mints and sends a per-tab identifier — which is (c)'s cooperation requirement under another name. Genuinely new state, with expiry, ownership and cleanup of its own. **(c) Make the switch an explicit act** — `POST /api/v1/me/workspace`, which audits and returns; `require_caller` audits nothing. Zero cost on ordinary requests, and the only option whose trigger granularity matches the act being audited, since the client is the only thing with tab granularity. `W6b-11`'s switcher already has the moment to call it. Cost: a client that only ever sends the header switches with no record — fatal under Reading 2, and under Reading 1 not a gap at all, because such a caller has not switched. |  |  |  |
| OQ-652 | open question |  | ~~**What tells the API that a workspace selection *changed*, so that a switch is audited once rather than never or on every request?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): Reading 1 — a switch is a human act; option (c).** Raised 2026-08-24 by W32-7. FR-396's fourth obligation says a switch is audited into both chains, and W32-7 built the mechanism — `platform.workspace_switch.record_switch` writes one event in the workspace left and one in the workspace entered, ordered by id so opposite-direction switches cannot deadlock on the per-workspace advisory lock. The call site is what is missing, and not for want of effort: `require_caller` runs once per request and holds no memory of the previous one, so "the selection changed" is not a fact it can observe. *(amended 2026-08-24, the day it was raised.)* **No mechanism is recommended**, because the choice is not derivable from the options: it turns on which reading of FR-396's fourth obligation is correct — a switch as a human act, or a switch as any difference in effective scope between two consecutive requests — and the two readings select different options. Storing the previous selection **on the user row** is refused under both: a single principal-level "current workspace" cannot represent two open tabs, so every request from either is recorded as a switch, two audit events and two per-workspace advisory locks apiece, in exactly the two-tab case FR-397 made the selection per-request to support. Auditing every selection is the option to refuse out loud under a human-act reading, because it needs no schema change and so is the one a later reader reaches for, and it turns the FR-372 chain into a request log. Options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). **Decided 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): a switch is a human act — option (c).** `POST /api/v1/me/workspace` audits via `record_switch` and returns; `require_caller` audits nothing; `W6b-11`'s switcher calls it on selection. Reading 2 would record every two-tab alternation as a stop that never happened, in the case `FR-397` chose per-request transport to support, and asks the platform to audit a fact it cannot observe. **`W6b-11b` vacates**; obligation 4's remaining work is the endpoint and the switcher call. |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-653 | open question |  | ~~**20 of the 39 `entity_ref` spellings the backend writes into the audit chain do not parse as an `ArtifactRef`, and nothing notices.** *(raised 2026-08-24 by W32-7 Task 4, which set out to ask a much smaller question and measured this instead.)* W32-7 writes `workspace:<slug>@1` for a switch event; `workspace` is not in `ARTIFACT_TYPES`, so `ArtifactRef.parse` rejects it. Measured on `backend/src` before recording that as the finding: **13 types other than `workspace` already appear in `entity_ref` and are not in the frozenset** — `actor`, `approval_policy`, `backtest`, `blob`, `job`, `model_comparison`, `model_family`, `principal`, `profile`, `role`, `service_account`, `setting`, `validation_report` — and **5 more use a listed type but carry no `@version`**, which `_REF_RE` also rejects: `dataset:{slug}`, `dataset_version:{version_id}`, `model:{model_id}`, `reference_table:{slug}`. Only 19 of 39 parse. `audit.record` and `AuditEvent` both type the column as a plain `str` and nothing parses it, which is why a majority-unparseable column has been invisible. The real question is therefore not whether to admit one more type: it is **what `entity_ref` is**, because the code has been answering "a name for whatever the event is about" while the type name answers "an `ArtifactRef`".~~ | **(a) Widen `ARTIFACT_TYPES` until every written ref parses.** 13 new types plus a fix for the 5 unversioned spellings, in `refs.py` and the mirrored `artifact-ref.schema.json`. It also redefines the frozenset as "things that appear in `entity_ref`" rather than "things that are artifacts" — and `00` §2 makes a Workspace, a Principal and a Role the *scope and subjects* artifacts exist under, not artifacts. `@1` on a workspace is a version that will never exist. **(b) Declare that `entity_ref` names the subject of the event, which is an `ArtifactRef` when the subject is an artifact and a scoped `type:name` otherwise**, and give it a validator of its own that admits both. Writes down the contract the code has held all along, costs one shared regex and a test, and leaves `ARTIFACT_TYPES` meaning what it says. Cost: a consumer that parses the column must handle two shapes. **(c) Split the column** — `entity_ref` stays an `ArtifactRef` and is nullable, with a second `subject` column for non-artifact events. Cleanest model and the most expensive: a migration over every stored audit event, in a table `06` FR-372 hash-chains, so a backfill is a chain rewrite. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — entity_ref names the subject of the event: an ArtifactRef when the subject is an artifact, a scoped type:name otherwise** |  |  |  |
| OQ-653 | open question |  | ~~**20 of the 39 `entity_ref` spellings the backend writes into the audit chain do not parse as an `ArtifactRef`, and nothing notices.**~~ Raised 2026-08-24 by W32-7, which set out to ask whether a switch event's `workspace:<slug>@1` should be admitted to `ARTIFACT_TYPES` and measured the column instead: 13 types besides `workspace` are already written and absent from the frozenset — `actor`, `approval_policy`, `backtest`, `blob`, `job`, `model_comparison`, `model_family`, `principal`, `profile`, `role`, `service_account`, `setting`, `validation_report` — and 5 more use a listed type with no `@version`. Only 19 of 39 parse. The column is typed as a plain `str` and nothing parses it, which is why a majority-unparseable column has been invisible. The question is therefore what `entity_ref` *is*: the code has held "a name for the subject of the event" while the type name says "an `ArtifactRef`". The recommendation is to declare and validate that first reading rather than widen `ARTIFACT_TYPES` to 13 new types, which would redefine the set as "things that appear in `entity_ref`" — and `00` §2 makes a Workspace, a Principal and a Role the scope and subjects artifacts exist under, not artifacts. Options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md). Carried either way: `ARTIFACT_TYPES` is never widened to make an existing string parse, and the 5 unversioned refs are a plain bug — `dataset:{slug}` records which dataset was touched but not which version, which is the fact an auditor came for. Owner: maintainer. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (b) — entity_ref names the subject of the event: an ArtifactRef when the subject is an artifact, a scoped type:name otherwise** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-654 | open question |  | ~~**`req-coverage.py`'s three inflation modes are not the three that were reported: one is already closed by another gate, one has a live instance the measurement could not see, and the one that is firing is unreachable by any change to the instrument.** Raised 2026-08-24 (WK-664), measured against `e2ae7c6`; baseline `523 specified · 269 marked · 51.4%`. The denominator is built from ids wrapped in markdown bold (`scripts/req-coverage.py:45`) and the numerator by regex over raw source text (`:51`), so *claimed* means only "this byte sequence occurs in a file named `test_*.py`". **(1) The denominator mode is already enforced elsewhere.** `scripts/audit-docs.py:332-360` builds `defined` from bold-only spec ids and `referenced` from a word-bounded FR/NFR id pattern over every markdown line, then fails on the difference — the proposed guard's predicate exactly. Proven on deliberately broken input: an unbolded requirement row exits 1, the identical row with four asterisks exits 0. **(2) The numerator mode has a live instance, and the measurement that reported zero could not have found it.** The reported figure was `0 of 1638` markers *outside decorator position*, but the requirement's predicate is a marker on something pytest **collects**. `backend/tests/test_celery_broker.py:66` satisfies the proxy and violates the predicate — a real `@pytest.mark.req("FR-406")` in true decorator position on the helper `async def _drain`, which pytest never collects — while the test it belonged to at `:96` carries no marker at all. An AST reader over all five `testpaths` roots rejects that one site of 1637 and loses no ids: 269 under regex, 269 under AST. **The site itself is repaired separately as a plain bug** — moving the decorator onto the collected test restores the traceability link and leaves this finding untouched, because the published figure reads 269 either way: the gap is that the instrument cannot tell the two arrangements apart, not that the count is wrong. **(3) The live mode is clause-conflation, and no instrument change reaches it**, because the unit is the id and the defect is inside the sentence. `FR-62` (`01-data-management.md:160`) is two clauses and its four markers evidence only the first. The per-requirement count is additionally **mislabelled**: `:47-48` appends one entry per marker *occurrence* and `:52` prints it as "N test file(s)".~~ | **(a) A standing reporting rule** — every closure record quoting the figure states it is a starting list, not a bound. Costs nothing, closes no mode; it is not a third option beside the code changes but their release note, and shipping guards without it is net-negative. Necessary but **not sufficient** for a §13 discharge, since a blanket caveat is not a verdict on `FR-62`'s second clause. **(b) Fail when an id appears in a spec and is never bolded — strike this.** At the only scope where it is correct it re-implements `audit-docs.py` checks 2 and 3, a second source of truth for one rule, which `CLAUDE.md` §2 forbids; at any narrower scope it fails on first run against 1188 unbolded id occurrences across 365 ids in `docs/specs/`, and `docs/workflows/` holds 160 id references with no bolded ids at all. **(c) Parse markers with `ast` and require a collected test** — accept a decorator only on a function pytest will collect and run, and print every rejected site rather than silently omitting it. Live yield today is one stranded marker and one test that lost its traceability link. Its residual risk is converting future divergent spellings from *silently counted* to *silently dropped*, which printing rejections mitigates and `--strict-markers` does not, since that validates a marker's name and not its host. **(d) Move the unit from requirement to clause** — reaches the live mode and nothing else does, but it is a spec-format *and* marker-format change across the corpus, and the split is not at sentence boundaries. Its useful signal is a subset of (e)'s. **(e) Warn when a requirement's text carries a UI clause and every one of its markers is backend.** Both operands come from files the script already opens, so it needs no frontend reader and never touches the VCS-ignored generated client. It reaches `FR-62`. It must be a **warning, never a failure**: requirements naming the UI as *evidence of absence* (`FR-84`, `FR-86`) are blast-radius measurements, and a keyword detector flags both. Its real cost is first-run volume — **31 requirements on day one**, which reads as noise until triaged. **(f) Print a truthful per-requirement count** — a set, and a label naming what it counts. Not an inflation mode; a quantity mislabelled as another quantity. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c), (e) and (f), with (a) as their release note; (b) struck** |  |  |  |
| OQ-654 | open question |  | ~~**`req-coverage.py`'s three inflation modes are not the three that were reported.**~~ Raised 2026-08-24 (WK-664) against `e2ae7c6`, baseline `523 specified · 269 marked · 51.4%`. The denominator mode is already enforced by `audit-docs.py` checks 2 and 3; the numerator mode has one live instance, a marker in true decorator position on a helper pytest never collects, so a test lost its traceability link and the instrument credited the loss to the helper; and the mode that is actually firing is **clause-conflation**, which no cheap instrument change reaches, because the unit is the requirement id and the defect is inside the sentence. Six options are in [`../open-questions.md`](open-questions.md), recommending an AST marker reader, a UI-clause warning and a truthful per-requirement count, with a standing reporting rule as their release note. Owner: maintainer. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c), (e) and (f), with (a) as their release note; (b) struck** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-655 | open question |  | ~~**Does the frontend need a request-shaped generated type, or does every write path cast?** `frontend/package.json`'s `generate:api` runs `openapi-typescript` `^7.13.0` with no flags, so `--default-non-nullable` takes its default of *true* and every property carrying a JSON-Schema `default` is emitted as a *required* TypeScript property. Verified in the artifact: `GlmSpec` renders `alpha: number` with no `?`, beside `approximates_model_id?: string \| null`. That is right for reading a response — the server always populates a default — and wrong for writing a request, where the client may omit it and the server fills it in. One generated type serves both directions. The generated OpenAPI is not at fault: `GlmSpec` lists 3 required and 13 defaulted properties with no property in both sets, and `EbmSpec` and `GbmSpec` likewise. The consequence today is three `as unknown as ModelSpec` casts at `frontend/src/views/ModelSpecBuilderView.vue`:133, :140 and :146 — the only non-test `as unknown as` in `frontend/src` — which disable compiler checking of every outgoing spec body, including field-name errors the cast was never aimed at. `W6b-4a` diagnosed it at :110-119 and declined to hand-write the defaults, which `CLAUDE.md` §2 forbids; what a slice cannot do is change the generator. Nothing in `docs/` records the flag or the asymmetry. Mirrored in `docs/specs/07-platform.md` §10.~~ | **(a) Status quo** — a documented `unknown`-mediated cast at each write site. No work, and every outgoing body stays unchecked; the pattern spreads to each remaining write path whose body carries defaults. **(b) `--default-non-nullable false`** — request bodies type correctly, and every server-defaulted *response* field becomes optional, so every read site across the whole frontend needs a guard or an assertion. A small correct-by-construction gain bought with a large loss. **(c) Two generated type sets** — run the generator twice over the same `docs/contracts/openapi/generated.json`, once with the flag and once without, and alias request-body types from the permissive set while responses keep the strict one. Both are generated and VCS-ignored, so neither hand-writes a contract shape and §2 is not engaged; the cost is one more `generate:api` step and one more ignored artifact. **(d) Test-time validation** of outgoing bodies against the accurate generated JSON Schema — complements any of the three and fixes nothing at compile time. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) — two generated type sets: strict for responses, permissive for request bodies** |  |  |  |
| OQ-655 | open question |  | ~~**Does the frontend need a request-shaped generated type, or does every write path cast?**~~ `generate:api` runs `openapi-typescript` `^7.13.0` with no flags, so `--default-non-nullable` takes its default of *true* and every property carrying a JSON-Schema `default` is emitted as a *required* TypeScript property — `GlmSpec` renders `alpha: number` with no `?` beside `approximates_model_id?: string \| null`. Right for reading a response, wrong for writing a request, and one generated type serves both directions. The generated OpenAPI is not at fault: `GlmSpec` lists 3 required and 13 defaulted properties with no property in both sets. The consequence today is three `as unknown as ModelSpec` casts in `ModelSpecBuilderView.vue` — the only non-test `as unknown as` in `frontend/src` — disabling compiler checking of every outgoing spec body. Options are the status quo cast, `--default-non-nullable false` at the cost of every response read, or a second generated type set aliased for request bodies only; the last is recommended, since both sets are generated and neither hand-writes a contract shape. **`WK-664` can own it** — frontend tooling, not backend work or a `02` §5.1 change. **Nothing is blocked meanwhile**: `W6b-4b` reuses the documented cast. Raised 2026-08-25 by the `W6b-4a` arbitration. **Open**, owned by the maintainer; options and evidence are in `docs/open-questions.md`. **DECIDED 2026-08-26: (c) — two generated type sets: strict for responses, permissive for request bodies** |  |  |  |  |
| OQ-656 | open question |  | ~~**How does the browser learn the OIDC issuer and its `client_id`?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): (a) — a new unauthenticated `GET /api/v1/auth/config`, now stated as `FR-394`.** `FR-393` specifies the flow — authorization code with PKCE, a public client, against *the same provider and discovery document the API verifies against* — but not how the SPA obtains the two values that flow cannot start without. A discovery document resolves the provider's *endpoints* once the issuer is known; it never carries a `client_id`, which is per-registration. **No `oidc_client_id` exists anywhere.** `backend/src/app/config.py:138-153` configures `oidc_issuer`, `oidc_audience`, `oidc_jwks_url`, `oidc_jwks_ttl_s` and `dev_auth_enabled`, and a sweep of `backend/src`, `frontend/src` and `packages` returns no other; `oidc_audience` is the resource server's own audience value, not a public client's registration id. `FR-398` already presupposes one — its checked-in realm defines *a public client with PKCE and no secret* — without naming where the platform configures it or how it reaches the browser. **The consequence is that the SPA contains no authentication code at all, in any mode** — a sweep of `frontend/src` outside `api/generated` returns no `Authorization`, `Bearer`, `PKCE` or `oidc`, and `frontend/src/api/client.ts:36-38` sends exactly three headers (`Accept`, `Content-Type`, `Idempotency-Key`), none of them a credential. `local`/`dev` works only because the **Vite dev proxy authenticates on the SPA's behalf**, injecting `x-dev-principal-id` from inside a `proxy.on("proxyReq")` handler (`frontend/vite.config.ts:64-67`) — the one path `FR-393` explicitly excludes from the flow, and one that vanishes from a built artifact. The API accepts three credentials and every one is a request header (`backend/src/app/api/deps.py:1-10`), so a built SPA presents none of them. **There is therefore nothing here for an implementer to extend.** **A cookie session is not the silent fallback it looks like:** `fetch` defaults to `credentials: "same-origin"`, so a cookie would be sent with no SPA code at all, but `backend/src/app` contains **zero cookie reads**, so nothing receives it. Every authenticated view is therefore unreachable in a deployed environment — the reachability question one layer down. And `/api/v1/settings` cannot be the carrier: `get_settings` takes `caller: ReadSettings` and resolves against `caller.workspace_id`, making it both chicken-and-egg and the wrong scope — an issuer is a deployment value, not a workspace setting. **Raised 2026-08-25 (WK-664), discharging a routing rather than duplicating it:** `docs/plans/PL-00793-w6b-14-the-local-oidc-provider-implementation-plan.md` Finding 3 records the same gap, routes it here as a `CLAUDE.md` §0 gap, and **deliberately mints no id** — *minting one in a plan would put an id in the suite that `open-questions.md` does not carry* — so the number is allocated here. That slice supplies the *values* and nothing supplies the *channel*; the plan books it **deferred, owner maintainer**, and records that it blocks **`W6b-10`** rather than W6b-14. | **(a) A new unauthenticated `GET /api/v1/auth/config`**, shaped in `model-schema` and generated onto both sides, carrying issuer, `client_id`, audience and `dev_auth_enabled`. The precedent is built: `/version` already publishes service name, version and environment unauthenticated (`backend/src/app/main.py:124-131`), beside `/healthz`, `/readyz` and `/metrics`. Cost: one endpoint and one contract shape, and it discloses the issuer and `client_id` to anonymous callers — though neither is a secret, since a public client's id travels in the authorize URL by construction. **(b) Build-time injection** — `VITE_OIDC_ISSUER` / `VITE_OIDC_CLIENT_ID` read through `import.meta.env`. Cost: it makes the built bundle deployment-specific, so one artifact cannot serve two deployments, and it states the issuer **twice** — once in the API's settings, once in the bundle — with nothing checking the two agree, which is the case `CLAUDE.md` §2 refuses. **`FR-437` independently forecloses it**: production runs on the deployer's own provider, so a value baked into the bundle is wrong for every deployment except the one that built it. It also opens a config channel that does not exist — `frontend/src/components/ChartFigure.vue:61` is the only `import.meta.env` use in the frontend, it reads `.DEV` (a Vite builtin), and there is no `.env` file at any level. **(c) Extend `/version`** with an auth block. Cheapest — an existing unauthenticated endpoint already publishing environment. Cost: it overloads a liveness and identity probe with an auth-bootstrap role, and `/version` is mounted outside the API prefix and outside the generated contract surface, so the SPA would hand-write the shape — which §2 forbids. |  |  |  |
| OQ-656 | open question |  | ~~**How does the browser learn the OIDC issuer and its `client_id`?**~~ **DECIDED 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): (a) — `FR-394` states the channel.** `FR-393` specifies the flow but not its bootstrap: a discovery document resolves the provider's endpoints once the issuer is known and never carries a `client_id`, and **no `oidc_client_id` exists anywhere** — `config.py` has `oidc_issuer`, `oidc_audience`, `oidc_jwks_url`, `oidc_jwks_ttl_s`, `dev_auth_enabled` and no other, `oidc_audience` being the resource server's audience rather than a client registration. `FR-398`'s realm already presupposes such a client without saying where it is configured. So the SPA carries **no authentication code at all, in any mode**: `local`/`dev` works only because the Vite dev proxy injects `x-dev-principal-id` on its behalf — the path `FR-393` excludes — and that vanishes from a built artifact, leaving nothing to extend. A built SPA presents none of the three header credentials the API accepts, and a cookie is not a silent fallback because nothing in `backend/src/app` reads one. `/api/v1/settings` cannot carry it — authenticated, and scoped to a workspace where an issuer is a deployment value. Options are a new unauthenticated `GET /api/v1/auth/config` on the `/version` precedent, build-time `import.meta.env` injection, or an auth block on `/version`; the first is recommended, the second states the issuer twice — `FR-437` forecloses it outright, since production runs on the deployer's own provider — and the third hand-writes a contract shape. Raised 2026-08-25 (WK-664), allocating the id that `docs/plans/PL-00793-w6b-14-the-local-oidc-provider-implementation-plan.md` Finding 3 routed here and deliberately left unminted; it blocks `W6b-10`. **Decided 2026-08-25 (WK-664 decision maker): (a) — an unauthenticated `GET /api/v1/auth/config`, stated as `FR-394`.** Options and evidence are in `docs/open-questions.md`. |  |  |  |  |

## Ownership matrix

| role | owns |
|---|---|
| decision-maker | requirement (FR/NFR/DEP), open question (OQ), workflow (WF), decision (ADR), ruling (RL) |
| maintainer | phase, work (WK), proposal (RFC), ruling (RL), reference: process/, reference: charters |
| planner | work (WK), slice (SL), plan (PL map/leaf) |
| executor | plan (PL handover), ledger (LG), research (RS spike/measurement), reference: contracts/ |
| auditor | plan (PL review), research (RS audit), closure (CR), finding (FD) |
| lead | phase, proposal (RFC), closure (CR), reference: skills, reference: agents |
| reporter |  |
| watcher |  |
